The tensor product is induced by the product of varieties, the unit object is the motive of a point, and duality is given by Poincaré duality (the Lefschetz motive L = h2(P1) is invertible, with inverse L−1). The fiber functor is given by any Weil cohomology theory (Betti, de Rham, ℓ-adic, crystalline), which assigns to a motive its cohomology vector space. The Tannaka group of this category is the motivic Galois group Gmot(k), a pro-reductive group over Q. The standard conjecture of Lefschetz type implies that the Lefschetz operator induces an isomorphism of motives, which ensures that the category is rigid. The conjecture that homological equivalence equals numerical equivalence implies that the category is semisimple (Jannsen's theorem: numerical equivalence is the finest adequate equivalence for which the category of motives is semisimple). The Hodge conjecture is equivalent to the statement that the fiber functor given by Betti cohomology is fully faithful on the subcategory of Hodge cycles. The Tate conjecture for a variety over a finite field is equivalent to the statement that the Frobenius element generates a dense subgroup of the center of the motivic Galois group of the variety. The period conjecture of Grothendieck (refined by André) states that the period isomorphism between Betti and de Rham cohomology is the only algebraic relation between periods; in Tannakian terms, this means the motivic Galois group is the full group of automorphisms of the fiber functor compatible with the Hodge filtration and the Frobenius action. The category of mixed motives (Voevodsky, Hanamura, Levine) is not semisimple; it is a Tannakian category with a weight filtration, and its Tannaka group is an extension of the motivic Galois group of pure motives by a pro-unipotent group. The Nori motives are constructed as the Tannakian category generated by the diagram of cohomology of algebraic varieties, without assuming the standard conjectures; the fiber functor is the Betti cohomology, and the Tannaka group is the Nori motivic Galois group.

The Tannakian Formalism for the Local Langlands Correspondence: The Category of Representations of the L-Group

The local Langlands correspondence for a reductive group G over a p-adic field F can be formulated in Tannakian terms. Let G∨ be the Langlands dual group (a complex reductive group). The category Rep(G∨) of finite-dimensional algebraic representations of G∨ is a neutral Tannakian category over C with fiber functor the forgetful functor to VectC. Its Tannaka group is G∨ itself. On the other side, the category of smooth irreducible representations of G(F) is not a tensor category. However, the Bernstein center of the category of smooth representations acts as a ring of endomorphisms of the identity functor. The local Langlands correspondence (proved for GLn by Harris-Taylor and Henniart, for classical groups by Arthur and Mok) establishes a bijection between irreducible smooth representations of G(F) and Langlands parameters, which are homomorphisms φ : WF × SL2(C) → G∨ satisfying certain conditions, where WF is the Weil group of F. In Tannakian language, a Langlands parameter φ defines a tensor functor from Rep(G∨) to the category of representations of WF × SL2(C), which is a Tannakian category over C. The L-packet associated to φ is the set of irreducible representations of G(F) corresponding to φ. The enhanced L-packet includes a character of the component group Sφ = π0(Cent(φ)). The local Langlands correspondence for GLn can be upgraded to an equivalence of Tannakian categories between the category of finite-length smooth representations of GLn(F) generated by supercuspidals (with a suitable tensor product given by the Bernstein-Zelevinsky product) and the category of representations of the Weil-Deligne group WF × SL2(C) with a certain monodromy operator. The geometric Langlands correspondence (proved for GLn by Gaitsgory, Arinkin, et al.) is a Tannakian equivalence between the category of D-modules on the moduli stack BunG of G-bundles on a curve X and the category of quasi-coherent sheaves on the moduli stack LocSysG∨ of G∨-local systems. The Hecke eigensheaf property translates to the statement that the Tannaka group of the category of D-modules on BunG is the Langlands dual group G∨. The categorical Langlands correspondence (Ben-Zvi, Sakuma, Nadler) formulates this as an equivalence of monoidal ∞-categories, where the Tannakian formalism is replaced by the theory of Eilenberg-Moore categories for the action of the Hecke category.

Tannakian Categories and the Theory of Periods: The Period Conjecture and the Motivic Galois Group

The ring of periods P is the subring of C generated by integrals of algebraic differential forms over algebraic cycles on algebraic varieties defined over Q̄. Kontsevich and Zagier conjectured that the algebraic relations between periods are exactly those coming from the standard rules of calculus (Stokes' theorem, change of variables, additivity of integrals). In the Tannakian framework, the category of mixed motives MM(Q) (assuming its existence with the expected properties) is a neutral Tannakian category over Q with two fiber functors: the Betti realization ωB (singular cohomology with Q-coefficients) and the de Rham realization ωdR (algebraic de Rham cohomology). The period isomorphism is the natural isomorphism ωB ⊗ C ≅ ωdR ⊗ C. The motivic Galois group Gmot is the automorphism group of the fiber functor ωB (or ωdR). The period conjecture of Grothendieck (in the form refined by André) states that the transcendence degree of the field generated by the periods of a motive M over Q equals the dimension of the Gmot-torsor of isomorphisms between ωB(M) and ωdR(M). Equivalently, the period matrix of M has no algebraic relations other than those forced by the Tannakian structure (tensor products, duals, and the comparison isomorphism). The Tannakian category of mixed Tate motives over Z (or a number field) is the subcategory generated by the Tate motive Q(n). Its Tannaka group is an extension of Gm by a pro-unipotent group whose Lie algebra is the free Lie algebra on generators in degrees 3, 5, 7, ... (the motivic Lie algebra). The periods of mixed Tate motives are multiple zeta values. The Brown's theorem on the motivic fundamental group of P1 {0, 1, ∞} shows that the category of mixed Tate motives over Z is generated by the motivic fundamental group, and the period conjecture for this category is equivalent to the algebraic independence of multiple zeta values over Q. The Nori motivic Galois group is constructed unconditionally as the Tannaka group of the category of Nori motives, which is defined as the category of representations of a certain pro-algebraic group acting on the diagram of cohomology of algebraic varieties. The period conjecture for Nori motives is a precise statement about the injectivity of the period map from the motivic Galois group to the group of automorphisms of the period matrix.

  • Confusing the Tannakian category of pure motives (semisimple, pro-reductive Tannaka group) with the category of mixed motives (non-semisimple, Tannaka group has pro-unipotent radical).
  • Assuming that the category of representations of a reductive group in positive characteristic is Tannakian; it is not closed under taking kernels in general, and the failure of Deligne's theorem means the symmetric monoidal structure does not come from a fiber functor to vector spaces.
  • Treating the Frobenius action on crystalline cohomology as a linear operator without the filtration; the Tannakian fiber functor Dcris lands in filtered φ-modules, and the weak admissibility condition involves both the Frobenius slopes and the Hodge filtration.
  • Believing that the local Langlands correspondence is a Tannakian equivalence for all reductive groups; it is known to be a bijection on irreducible representations, but the Tannakian structure (tensor product of representations) is only fully understood for GLn and in the geometric setting.
  • Overlooking that the period conjecture is a statement about the motivic Galois group, not just about individual periods; it asserts that the period torsor is a principal homogeneous space under the motivic Galois group.
  • Confusing the Nori motivic Galois group (constructed unconditionally via diagrams of cohomology) with the Grothendieck motivic Galois group (which assumes the standard conjectures for pure motives); they coincide on the subcategory of mixed Tate motives but differ in general.
  • Neglecting that the category of F-isocrystals is Tannakian over the fraction field of Witt vectors, not over the base field of characteristic p; the Frobenius is a σ-linear operator, and the Tannakian formalism requires extending scalars to a field of characteristic zero.

Tannakian Categories and the Theory of Motives over Function Fields: The Geometric Langlands Correspondence

Let X be a smooth projective curve over a finite field 𝔽_q or over ℂ. The geometric Langlands program predicts an equivalence between the derived category of D‑modules on the moduli stack Bun_G of G‑bundles on X and the derived category of quasi‑coherent sheaves on the stack LocSys^L G of ^L G‑local systems, where ^L G is the Langlands dual group. In the Tannakian setting one works with the abelian category of constructible ℓ‑adic sheaves (or D‑modules) on Bun_G equipped with the convolution product given by the Hecke correspondences. This monoidal category is rigid and admits a fiber functor to vector spaces given by taking the stalk at the trivial bundle. The Tannaka group of this neutral Tannakian category is precisely the Langlands dual group ^L G, and the equivalence of categories is a Tannakian equivalence. The geometric Satake equivalence identifies the spherical Hecke category with Rep(^L G), providing the crucial link between the Hecke operators and the representation theory of the dual group. Over function fields the Frobenius action on ℓ‑adic cohomology yields a Weil group representation, and the Tannakian formalism translates the Langlands correspondence into an isomorphism of Tannaka groups. This perspective makes the compatibility of the correspondence with tensor products, duals, and the external product on curves transparent.

The construction of the fiber functor uses the fact that the trivial G‑bundle gives a point in Bun_G, and the stalk functor at this point is exact and monoidal. The Tannaka reconstruction then recovers ^L G as the automorphism group of this fiber functor. The Hecke eigensheaf condition for an automorphic sheaf F on Bun_G translates into the statement that the action of the spherical Hecke category on F factors through the fiber functor, i.e. F is a module over the Tannakian category Rep(^L G). The geometric Langlands equivalence is then an equivalence of module categories over Rep(^L G). Recent work of Gaitsgory, Rozenblyum, and others has extended this to a fully faithful embedding of the category of D‑modules on Bun_G into the category of ind‑coherent sheaves on LocSys^L G, and the Tannakian formalism provides the conceptual framework for proving that this embedding respects the monoidal structures.

Tannakian Categories and the Theory of Mixed Motives: The Weight Filtration and the Motivic t‑Structure

In the conjectural abelian category of mixed motives MM(k) over a field k of characteristic zero, every object M carries an increasing weight filtration W_•M such that the graded pieces gr^W_n M are pure motives of weight n. The existence of a motivic t‑structure on the triangulated category of motives DM(k) would have as its heart precisely MM(k), and the weight filtration would be the filtration induced by the t‑structure. The Tannakian formalism applies to the subcategory of mixed motives generated by a given motive M; its Tannaka group G_M is an extension of a pro‑reductive group (the motivic Galois group of the pure part) by a pro‑unipotent group encoding the extensions between different weights. The weight filtration is characterized by the property that the associated graded functor gr^W is a tensor functor to the category of pure motives, which is semisimple Tannakian. Consequently the pro‑unipotent radical of G_M acts trivially on gr^W, and the Lie algebra of the unipotent radical is graded by negative weights. This grading is reflected in the Ext groups Ext1MM(k)(ℚ(0), ℚ(n)) which vanish for n ≤ 0 and are finite‑dimensional for n > 0, giving the motivic Lie algebra. The Tannakian description of the weight filtration is essential for formulating the period conjecture: the period torsor of M is a G_M‑torsor, and its dimension equals the transcendence degree of the field generated by the periods of M.

Deligne’s construction of the category of mixed Hodge structures provides a concrete model: the weight filtration there is defined by the Hodge filtration and the weight filtration on the underlying rational vector space. The Tannaka group of the category of mixed Hodge structures is the semi‑direct product of the Mumford–Tate group of the pure part with a pro‑unipotent group whose Lie algebra is the free Lie algebra on the Ext groups. In the motivic setting one expects an analogous description, with the motivic Galois group replacing the Mumford–Tate group. The existence of the motivic t‑structure would imply that the weight filtration is functorial and compatible with tensor products, duals, and the six operations. This compatibility is precisely what makes the category of mixed motives a neutral Tannakian category over ℚ with the Betti or de Rham fiber functor.

Identifying the weight filtration in a mixed motive
Let M be a mixed motive over ℚ whose weight graded pieces are gr^W0 M ≅ ℚ(0), gr^W-2 M ≅ ℚ(1), and all other graded pieces zero. Suppose the extension class in Ext1MM(ℚ)(ℚ(0), ℚ(1)) corresponding to M is non‑zero. Determine the Tannaka group G_M and describe its action on the weight filtration.
The category generated by M is a neutral Tannakian category with fiber functor ω_B. Its Tannaka group G_M fits into an exact sequence 1 → U → G_M → G_m → 1, where G_m is the Tannaka group of the pure part ℚ(0) ⊕ ℚ(1) (the grading torus) and U is a pro‑unipotent group of dimension one, corresponding to the one‑dimensional Ext group. The Lie algebra of U is concentrated in weight -2, so U ≅ G_a as a group scheme. The action of G_m on U is by the character t ↦ t-2. Concretely, G_M ≅ G_a ⋊ G_m with multiplication (u, t)·(u', t') = (u + t-2u', tt'). The weight filtration is the filtration by the subgroups W-2 = U and W0 = G_M. The action of G_M on the fiber ω_B(M) respects this filtration: U acts trivially on gr^W0 and by translation on gr^W-2, while G_m acts by the weight characters 0 and -2 respectively.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question asks for the Tannaka group of a mixed motive with given weight graded pieces, first write down the pure Tannaka group (a torus), then add a unipotent radical for each non‑zero Ext group. Remember that the weight of the extension determines the G_m‑action on the unipotent part. Sketch the semi‑direct product and verify that the weight filtration corresponds to the filtration by the unipotent radical.

Tannakian Categories and the Theory of Periods: The Algebraic Independence of Multiple Zeta Values

Multiple zeta values (MZVs) are real numbers defined by nested sums ζ(n1,…,n_r) = Σk1>…>k_r≥1 k1-n1⋯k_r-n_r with n1≥2. They appear as periods of mixed Tate motives over ℤ, specifically as coefficients of the Drinfeld associator in the motivic fundamental group of ℙ1{0,1,∞}. The Tannakian category of mixed Tate motives over ℤ, denoted MTM(ℤ), is a neutral Tannakian category over ℚ whose Tannaka group G_MT is an extension of G_m by a pro‑unipotent group U whose Lie algebra is the free Lie algebra on generators σ2k+1 in degrees 3,5,7,…. The period isomorphism ω_B ⊗ ℂ ≅ ω_dR ⊗ ℂ identifies the Betti and de Rham realizations, and the period matrix of the motivic fundamental group has entries that are precisely the MZVs. Brown’s theorem (2012) proves that the category MTM(ℤ) is generated by the motivic fundamental group of ℙ1{0,1,∞}, and that the period conjecture for this category is equivalent to the statement that the MZVs are algebraically independent over ℚ modulo the known relations coming from the double shuffle and associator equations. In Tannakian terms, the period torsor Isom^⊗(ω_B, ω_dR) is a G_MT‑torsor, and its dimension equals the transcendence degree of the field generated by all MZVs. The known relations among MZVs correspond exactly to the Tannakian constraints: tensor products, duals, and the comparison isomorphism. No further algebraic relations are expected, which is the content of the period conjecture for mixed Tate motives.

The depth‑graded Lie algebra of U is isomorphic to the Lie algebra of the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group, and the action of the motivic Galois group on the motivic fundamental group factors through this quotient. The Tannakian formalism thus translates deep questions about special values of the Riemann zeta function into structural properties of a pro‑algebraic group. For instance, the dimension of the space of MZVs of weight n is conjectured to follow the Fibonacci‑like recurrence d_n = dn-2 + dn-3, which matches the dimensions of the graded pieces of the free Lie algebra on odd generators. This combinatorial prediction is a direct consequence of the Tannakian description of G_MT.

Tannakian Categories and the Theory of Motives: The Motivic Galois Group and the Hodge Conjecture

The Hodge conjecture asserts that every Hodge class on a smooth projective variety X over ℂ is a ℚ‑linear combination of classes of algebraic cycles. In the Tannakian language, let M be the pure motive h(X) in the category of pure motives (assuming the standard conjectures). The Hodge realization gives a fiber functor ω_Hdg to the category of polarizable Hodge structures, and the Betti realization gives ω_B to ℚ‑vector spaces. The Mumford–Tate group MT(M) is the Tannaka group of the Tannakian subcategory generated by M inside Hodge structures. The motivic Galois group G_mot(M) is the Tannaka group of the same subcategory inside motives. The comparison isomorphism ω_B ⊗ ℂ ≅ ω_Hdg ⊗ ℂ identifies the two fiber functors after extending scalars, yielding an inclusion G_mot(M) MT(M) × G_m (the extra G_m accounts for the Tate twist). The Hodge conjecture for X is equivalent to the statement that this inclusion is an equality after taking the derived subgroup, i.e. that every Hodge class comes from an algebraic cycle, which means the motivic Galois group is as large as the Hodge‑theoretic constraints allow. In particular, if the Hodge conjecture holds for all varieties, then for any pure motive M the natural map G_mot(M) → MT(M) is surjective onto the derived subgroup of MT(M). Conversely, a failure of surjectivity would produce a Hodge class not motivated by algebraic cycles. This Tannakian reformulation makes the Hodge conjecture a statement about the size of the motivic Galois group relative to the Mumford–Tate group, and it connects the conjecture to the period conjecture: the period torsor of M is a G_mot(M)‑torsor, and its dimension is bounded by the dimension of the MT(M)‑torsor of Hodge classes.

Deligne’s theorem on absolute Hodge classes shows that every absolute Hodge class is motivated, which in Tannakian terms means that the inclusion G_mot(M) → MT(M) is an isomorphism on the pro‑reductive quotient. The remaining discrepancy lies in the unipotent radical, which corresponds to extensions between motives of different weights. The Hodge conjecture for pure motives concerns only the reductive part, while the full period conjecture involves the unipotent part as well. This distinction explains why the Hodge conjecture is a statement about algebraic cycles, whereas the period conjecture is a statement about transcendence degrees of periods.

Tannakian Categories and the Theory of Motives: The Relative Motivic Galois Group over a Base Scheme

Let S be a regular Noetherian scheme. One can consider the category of motives over S, denoted MM(S), which is expected to be a neutral Tannakian category over ℚ with a fiber functor given by the étale realization at a geometric point of S. The Tannaka group of MM(S) is the relative motivic Galois group G_mot(S). When S = Spec(k) for a field k, this recovers the absolute motivic Galois group. For a general base, the relative group fits into an exact sequence 1 → G_mot(S) → G_mot(η) → π1ét(S) → 1, where η is the generic point of S and π1ét(S) is the étale fundamental group. This sequence reflects the fact that a motive over S gives a motive over the generic point together with a monodromy action of the fundamental group. The Tannakian formalism makes this precise: the fiber functor on MM(S) factors as the composition of the pullback to the generic point and the fiber functor on MM(η), and the automorphism group of this composite functor is the semi‑direct product of the absolute motivic Galois group of the generic point with the fundamental group acting via the monodromy representation. The weight filtration on motives over S is compatible with this sequence, and the graded pieces are pure motives over S whose Tannaka groups are extensions of the motivic Galois groups of the fibers by the fundamental group. This relative perspective is essential for the formulation of the motivic Langlands correspondence over function fields and for the study of motivic sheaves on Shimura varieties.

In the case where S is a Shimura variety, the relative motivic Galois group is closely related to the Mumford–Tate group of the universal abelian variety. The Tannakian category of motives over S generated by the universal abelian scheme has Tannaka group equal to the Mumford–Tate group of the generic fiber, extended by the fundamental group of S. This provides a motivic interpretation of the Hodge‑theoretic properties of Shimura varieties and connects the Tannakian formalism to the theory of canonical models and the Langlands–Kottwitz method. The relative motivic Galois group also appears in the work of Nori on the fundamental group scheme of a scheme, where the category of Nori motives over S is defined as the category of representations of a pro‑algebraic group acting on the diagram of cohomology of varieties over S. The Tannaka group of this category is precisely the relative motivic Galois group, and the exact sequence above is realized as the sequence of fundamental group schemes.

  • Confusing the absolute motivic Galois group of a field with the relative motivic Galois group over a base scheme; the latter includes monodromy from the fundamental group.
  • Assuming the weight filtration on mixed motives splits canonically; it does not, and the extension data is encoded in the unipotent radical of the Tannaka group.
  • Treating the period conjecture as a statement about individual periods rather than about the dimension of the period torsor as a G_mot‑torsor.
  • Believing that the geometric Langlands correspondence is an equivalence of abelian categories; it is an equivalence of derived (or ∞‑) categories, and the Tannakian structure lives on the heart of a t‑structure.
  • Overlooking that the Tannaka group of mixed Tate motives over ℤ is an extension of G_m by a pro‑unipotent group whose Lie algebra is free on odd generators; the even generators do not appear.
  • Neglecting that the Hodge conjecture is equivalent to the surjectivity of the motivic Galois group onto the derived subgroup of the Mumford–Tate group, not to an isomorphism of the full groups.
  • Assuming that the category of motives over a finite field is Tannakian with the same fiber functor as in characteristic zero; the Frobenius action forces a σ‑linear structure and the Tannakian formalism requires extending scalars to characteristic zero.

The Tannakian Category of (φ, Γ)-Modules and the p-adic Local Langlands Correspondence

The category of étale (φ, Γ)-modules over the Robba ring ℛℚ_p provides a fully faithful Tannakian description of the category of p-adic Galois representations of Gℚ_p. The fiber functor is given by taking the underlying ℚ_p-vector space of the (φ, Γ)-module, which is exact and faithful. The Tannaka group of this neutral Tannakian category is canonically isomorphic to the absolute Galois group Gℚ_p equipped with its natural profinite topology, and the tensor structure corresponds to the tensor product of Galois representations. Fontaine's equivalence of categories identifies the subcategory of crystalline (φ, Γ)-modules with the category of crystalline representations, and the subcategory of de Rham (φ, Γ)-modules with the category of de Rham representations. The p-adic local Langlands correspondence for GL2(ℚ_p) can be formulated entirely inside this Tannakian framework: the functor that attaches to a two-dimensional irreducible p-adic representation of Gℚ_p the corresponding unitary Banach space representation of GL2(ℚ_p) is a tensor functor between the Tannakian category of (φ, Γ)-modules and the category of admissible locally analytic representations of GL2(ℚ_p). The compatibility of this functor with the fiber functors on both sides reflects the equality of the associated Tannaka groups, which is the precise Tannakian incarnation of the local Langlands correspondence. The theory of trianguline (φ, Γ)-modules extends the Tannakian formalism to non‑semistable representations, and the associated (φ, Γ)-modules carry a natural filtration whose graded pieces are rank‑one (φ, Γ)-modules classified by characters of ℚ_p^×. This filtration corresponds under the Tannakian duality to the weight filtration on the Galois side, and the extension data is encoded in the unipotent radical of the Tannaka group of the category of trianguline modules.

The Tannakian Category of Mixed Hodge Modules and Saito's Theory

Saito's theory of mixed Hodge modules provides a Tannakian category MHM(X) for any complex algebraic variety X, which is a neutral Tannakian category over ℚ when X is smooth and projective. The fiber functor is given by the underlying perverse sheaf with its rational structure, or equivalently by the de Rham realization with its Hodge and weight filtrations. The Tannaka group of MHM(X) is a pro‑algebraic group that fits into an exact sequence 1 → U → GMHM(X) → GHS(X) → 1, where GHS(X) is the Tannaka group of the category of pure Hodge structures on X (the Mumford–Tate group of the cohomology) and U is a pro‑unipotent group encoding the extension data between pure Hodge modules of different weights. The weight filtration on mixed Hodge modules corresponds to the weight filtration on the Tannaka group, and the graded pieces are pure Hodge modules whose Tannaka groups are reductive. The six operations (f_*, f^!, f_!, f^*, ⊗, ℋom) on mixed Hodge modules are compatible with the Tannakian structure: they are tensor functors between the corresponding Tannakian categories, and the associated morphisms of Tannaka groups reflect the functoriality of the motivic Galois group. In particular, the nearby and vanishing cycle functors give rise to monodromy operators on the Tannaka group, and the monodromy theorem of Schmid translates into the statement that the logarithm of the monodromy operator is nilpotent on the Lie algebra of the Tannaka group. The category of mixed Hodge modules on a point is equivalent to the category of mixed Hodge structures, whose Tannaka group is the motivic Galois group of mixed Tate motives over ℚ when restricted to the subcategory generated by Tate objects. The Tannakian formalism for mixed Hodge modules thus provides a Hodge‑theoretic model for the category of mixed motives, and the comparison isomorphisms between Betti, de Rham, and ℓ-adic realizations become isomorphisms of fiber functors on the same Tannakian category.

Identifying the Tannakian Group of a Category of (φ, Γ)-Modules
Let ℛ be the Robba ring over ℚ_p. Consider the neutral Tannakian category Repℚ_p(Gℚ_p) of continuous finite-dimensional p-adic representations of the absolute Galois group Gℚ_p, and the category Modét(φ, Γ) of étale (φ, Γ)-modules over ℛ. Fontaine's equivalence gives a tensor equivalence F: Repℚ_p(Gℚ_p) → Modét(φ, Γ). Show that the Tannaka group of Modét(φ, Γ) with the fiber functor ω(M) = M (forgetful to ℚ_p-vector spaces) is canonically isomorphic to Gℚ_p.
The fiber functor ω on Modét(φ, Γ) is the composition of the inverse equivalence F-1 with the standard fiber functor ωGal on Repℚ_p(Gℚ_p) that forgets the Gℚ_p-action. Since F is a tensor equivalence, it induces an isomorphism of Tannaka groups Aut^⊗(ω) ≅ Aut^⊗(ωGal). By Tannaka duality for the neutral Tannakian category Repℚ_p(Gℚ_p), the group Aut^⊗(ωGal) is canonically isomorphic to Gℚ_p (as a profinite group, viewed as a pro‑algebraic group over ℚ_p). Therefore Aut^⊗(ω) ≅ Gℚ_p. The isomorphism is canonical because the equivalence F is canonical (Fontaine's functor Drig^†). Hence the Tannaka group of the category of étale (φ, Γ)-modules is exactly the absolute Galois group of ℚ_p.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the Tannaka group of a category defined via an equivalence, always trace the fiber functor through the equivalence. The Tannaka group is invariant under tensor equivalences that commute with the fiber functors. Write down the composition of functors explicitly and invoke the known Tannaka group of the source category. This avoids recomputing automorphisms from scratch.

The Pro‑Algebraic Completion of the Topological Fundamental Group and the Tannakian Category of Local Systems

For a connected topological space X with a base point x, the category LocSys_ℚ(X) of finite-dimensional ℚ-local systems on X is a neutral Tannakian category over ℚ with fiber functor given by the stalk at x. Its Tannaka group π1alg(X, x) is the pro‑algebraic completion of the discrete fundamental group π1(X, x). Concretely, π1alg(X, x) is the affine group scheme over ℚ representing the functor that sends a ℚ-algebra R to the set of group homomorphisms π1(X, x) → G(R) for every affine group scheme G over ℚ. The natural map π1(X, x) → π1alg(X, x)(ℚ) is universal among homomorphisms from π1(X, x) to the ℚ-points of affine group schemes. When X is a smooth complex algebraic variety, the Riemann–Hilbert correspondence identifies LocSys_ℚ(X) with the category of regular holonomic D-modules on X, and the Tannaka group π1alg(X, x) coincides with the Tannaka group of the category of regular holonomic D-modules. The weight filtration on the pro‑algebraic fundamental group arises from the mixed Hodge structure on the cohomology of X: the graded pieces are the pro‑algebraic completions of the fundamental groups of the successive quotients of the weight filtration on the cohomology. In the case where X is a curve of genus g ≥ 2, the pro‑algebraic fundamental group is a pro‑unipotent extension of the pro‑reductive group Sp2g (the Mumford–Tate group of the Jacobian) by a pro‑unipotent group whose Lie algebra is the free Lie algebra on 2g generators modulo the single relation given by the symplectic form. The Tannakian formalism thus translates topological properties of X into algebraic properties of π1alg(X, x), and the comparison with the étale fundamental group is given by the natural map π1ét(X, x) → π1alg(X, x)(ℚ_ℓ) induced by the ℓ-adic realization functor.

The Unipotent Radical of the Motivic Galois Group and the Extension Groups of Mixed Motives

Let MM(k) be the conjectural abelian category of mixed motives over a field k of characteristic zero, assumed to be a neutral Tannakian category over ℚ with fiber functor given by the Betti realization. Its Tannaka group Gmot(k) is the motivic Galois group. The weight filtration on mixed motives induces a weight filtration on Gmot(k) whose graded quotients are the motivic Galois groups of the categories of pure motives of a given weight. The unipotent radical Umot(k) of Gmot(k) is a pro‑unipotent group whose Lie algebra is graded by negative weights. The Lie algebra Lie(Umot(k)) is canonically isomorphic to the direct sum over n ≥ 1 of Ext1MM(k)(ℚ(0), ℚ(n))^∨, where ℚ(n) denotes the Tate motive of weight -2n. In other words, the extension groups between Tate motives control the unipotent radical. For mixed Tate motives over ℤ, the Ext1 groups are known: Ext1MTM(ℤ)(ℚ(0), ℚ(n)) is one-dimensional for odd n ≥ 3 and zero for even n. Consequently, the Lie algebra of the unipotent radical of the motivic Galois group of mixed Tate motives over ℤ is the free Lie algebra on generators σ2n+1 in degree -(2n+1) for n ≥ 1. The extension data for non‑Tate mixed motives is more complicated, but the general principle remains: the unipotent radical encodes all non‑split extensions between pure motives of different weights. The period conjecture of Grothendieck predicts that the dimension of the period torsor of a mixed motive M equals the dimension of the Gmot(k)-torsor of isomorphisms between the Betti and de Rham fiber functors restricted to the Tannakian subcategory generated by M. The unipotent radical contributes precisely the transcendental periods arising from iterated integrals, and the algebraic independence of these periods is equivalent to the freeness of the corresponding Lie algebra generators.

The Period Torsor as a G_mot‑Torsor and the Grothendieck Period Conjecture

For a neutral Tannakian category C over ℚ with two fiber functors ω_B (Betti) and ωdR (de Rham), the set of tensor isomorphisms Iso^⊗(ω_B, ωdR) is a torsor under the Tannaka group G = Aut^⊗(ω_B). In the category of mixed motives over a field k, this torsor is the period torsor Pmot(k). The Grothendieck period conjecture asserts that the natural map from the set of periods of motives (the entries of the period matrix) to the coordinate ring of Pmot(k) is an isomorphism, i.e., the periods are algebraically independent over ℚ exactly to the extent dictated by the Tannakian structure. Equivalently, the transcendence degree of the field generated by the periods of a motive M equals the dimension of the Gmot(k)-orbit of the comparison isomorphism between ω_B(M) and ωdR(M). For mixed Tate motives over ℤ, the period torsor is an affine space whose coordinates are the multiple zeta values ζ(2n+1). The conjecture predicts that the odd zeta values are algebraically independent over ℚ, and that all algebraic relations among multiple zeta values are generated by the double shuffle relations. The Tannakian formalism makes this precise: the Lie algebra of the unipotent radical of GmotMT(ℤ) is free on generators corresponding to the odd zeta values, and the period torsor is the exponential of this Lie algebra. The conjecture for general mixed motives involves the full motivic Galois group, including its reductive part. The Hodge conjecture for pure motives is equivalent to the surjectivity of the map Gmot(k) → MT(M) onto the derived subgroup of the Mumford–Tate group, which ensures that the period torsor for pure motives has the expected dimension. The period conjecture thus unifies the Hodge conjecture (reductive part) and the algebraic independence of periods (unipotent part) into a single Tannakian statement about the dimension of the period torsor.

  • Confusing the Tannaka group of (φ, Γ)-modules with the Galois group of the base field; the equivalence is canonical but the fiber functor must be tracked carefully.
  • Assuming the category of mixed Hodge modules is Tannakian over ℚ for singular varieties; it is only neutral Tannakian when the variety is smooth and projective, otherwise the fiber functor may not be exact.
  • Treating the pro‑algebraic completion of the topological fundamental group as a profinite group; it is an affine group scheme over ℚ, not a profinite group, and its ℚ-points are not the étale fundamental group.
  • Believing the unipotent radical of the motivic Galois group is always free; it is free only for mixed Tate motives over ℤ, while for general mixed motives it has relations coming from the geometry of the base field.
  • Interpreting the period conjecture as a statement about individual periods rather than about the dimension of the period torsor as a G_mot‑torsor.
  • Neglecting that the comparison isomorphism between Betti and de Rham realizations is a point of the period torsor, and that the Grothendieck period conjecture is precisely the statement that this point is generic.

The Tannakian Formalism for the Category of Mixed Motives over a Number Field: The Bloch–Kato Conjecture and the Motivic Galois Group

The category of mixed motives over a number field K is expected to be a neutral Tannakian category over ℚ once a suitable fiber functor (for instance the Betti realization after fixing an embedding K ℂ) is chosen. The Tannaka group of this fiber functor is the motivic Galois group Gmot(K). The Bloch–Kato conjecture, now a theorem in many cases thanks to the work of Voevodsky, Rost, and others, predicts a precise description of the Galois cohomology groups Hi(GK, ℚp(j)) in terms of motivic cohomology. In the Tannakian language this translates into a statement about the Lie algebra of the unipotent radical of Gmot(K1 between Tate motives ℚ(0) and ℚ(n) are identified with the Bloch–Kato Selmer groups. The conjecture asserts that the natural map from motivic cohomology to Galois cohomology is an isomorphism after tensoring with ℚp, which in Tannakian terms means that the Lie algebra of the unipotent radical is freely generated by the classes in Ext1 that survive the comparison isomorphism. For a number field K the reductive quotient of Gmot(KK in terms of Galois representations, linking the abstract Tannakian reconstruction to explicit arithmetic invariants such as special values of L-functions and the structure of Selmer groups.

In practice, the Tannakian formalism allows one to transfer questions about motives to questions about representations of Gmot(KM and N of weights w and w+1 is equivalent to the non‑vanishing of a certain Ext1 group in the Tannakian category, which by the Bloch–Kato conjecture corresponds to a non‑zero element in a Selmer group. This perspective has been used to prove cases of the Beilinson conjectures on special values of L-functions by interpreting the regulator map as a period isomorphism between the Betti and de Rham fiber functors restricted to the Tannakian subcategory generated by the relevant motives. The Tannakian group Gmot(KK through the Bloch–Kato isomorphism.

The Tannakian Category of Representations of the Motivic Fundamental Group of a Smooth Projective Variety

Let X be a smooth projective variety over a field k of characteristic zero. The motivic fundamental group π1mot(X, x) is defined as the Tannaka group of the neutral Tannakian category of unipotent vector bundles with integrable connection on X (or, equivalently, the category of unipotent variations of mixed Hodge structure on X when k = ℂ). This category is a full subcategory of the category of mixed motives over X and inherits a fiber functor given by the fiber at the base point x. The resulting affine group scheme π1mot(X, x) is a pro‑unipotent extension of the usual topological fundamental group π1(X(ℂ), x) by a pro‑unipotent group whose Lie algebra is graded by the weight filtration. The Tannakian formalism identifies the category of finite‑dimensional representations of π1mot(X, x) with the category of unipotent vector bundles with connection, and the weight filtration on representations corresponds to the length of the unipotent filtration. In particular, the graded pieces of the Lie algebra of the unipotent radical are canonically isomorphic to the spaces of iterated integrals on X, which are periods of mixed motives on X. This construction generalizes the classical Riemann–Hilbert correspondence: the de Rham fiber functor yields the category of unipotent connections, while the Betti fiber functor yields the category of local systems. The comparison isomorphism between these two fiber functors is a torsor under π1mot(X, x), and its coordinates are precisely the multiple polylogarithms on X. The Tannakian approach thus provides a conceptual framework for the study of periods of mixed motives on varieties, linking the geometry of X (through its fundamental group) to the arithmetic of periods via the motivic Galois group.

An important consequence is that the motivic fundamental group fits into an exact sequence 1 → U → π1mot(X, x) → π1(X(ℂ), x) → 1, where U is a pro‑unipotent group whose Lie algebra is the free Lie algebra on the generators given by the cohomology of X in degree one, modulo the relations coming from the cup product. This description is the motivic analogue of the classical result of Chen on iterated integrals. The Tannakian category of representations of π1mot(X, x) is therefore equivalent to the category of unipotent variations of mixed Hodge structure on X, and the weight filtration on this category coincides with the filtration by the length of the unipotent monodromy. This equivalence is a cornerstone of the modern theory of mixed motives and provides a bridge between the topological fundamental group and the motivic Galois group of the base field.

Worked Example: Computing the Unipotent Radical of the Motivic Fundamental Group of ℙ¹ {0,1,∞}
Let X = ℙ¹ {0,1,∞} over ℂ. The topological fundamental group is the free group on two generators γ₀, γ₁. Describe the Lie algebra of the unipotent radical of π1mot(X, x) and identify its generators in terms of iterated integrals.
The category of unipotent vector bundles with connection on X is neutral Tannakian with fiber functor the fiber at a base point x. The Tannaka group π1mot(X, x) fits into 1 → U → π1mot → F₂ → 1, where F₂ is the free group on two generators. The Lie algebra Lie(U) is the free Lie algebra on two generators e₀, e₁ corresponding to the cohomology classes dual to γ₀, γ₁. The weight grading is given by the length of Lie words: e₀, e₁ have weight −1; brackets [e₀, e₁] have weight −2, etc. The iterated integrals ∫γ ω₁⋯ωn with ωi ∈ {dlog z, dlog(1−z)} correspond to the coordinates on the torsor of comparison isomorphisms, and the coefficients of the expansion of the KZ‑connection give the canonical generators of Lie(U). Thus the unipotent radical is the free pro‑unipotent group on two generators, and its Lie algebra is the free Lie algebra on e₀, e₁.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the Tannakian group of a category of unipotent objects, first identify the fiber functor (usually the fiber at a point). Then write down the exact sequence relating the motivic fundamental group to the topological fundamental group and the unipotent radical. The Lie algebra of the unipotent radical is almost always a free (or quotient of a free) Lie algebra on the degree‑one cohomology generators; remember that the weight grading is by length of Lie words. This pattern appears repeatedly in problems about periods of mixed motives on curves.

The Tannakian Formalism for the Category of p-adic Hodge Theory: Filtered φ-Modules and the Category of Crystalline Representations

Let K be a finite extension of ℚp. The category of crystalline representations of the absolute Galois group GK is a neutral Tannakian category over ℚp with the fiber functor given by the forgetful functor to ℚp-vector spaces (or, equivalently, the functor Dcris taking a representation to its associated filtered φ‑module). Fontaine’s equivalence of categories identifies crystalline representations with weakly admissible filtered φ‑modules over K. This equivalence is a tensor equivalence, hence it respects the Tannakian structure. The Tannaka group of the fiber functor on the category of crystalline representations is the pro‑algebraic completion of the image of GK in the automorphism group of the fiber functor; it can be described as the group of tensor automorphisms of the functor Dcris. In concrete terms, a filtered φ‑module is a finite‑dimensional K₀-vector space D equipped with a Frobenius semilinear automorphism φ and a decreasing filtration on D ⊗K₀ K. The weak admissibility condition (Newton polygon above Hodge polygon) is exactly the condition that the corresponding representation be crystalline. The Tannakian formalism then allows one to reconstruct the Galois representation from its filtered φ‑module by taking the Tannaka dual of the subcategory generated by that object. Moreover, the category of all de Rham representations is also Tannakian, and the inclusion of crystalline representations corresponds to the condition that the Hodge–Tate weights are integral and the filtration is split after a finite extension. The Tannakian group of the de Rham category is the pro‑algebraic completion of GK with respect to all de Rham representations, and its quotient by the unipotent radical is the product of the reductive groups attached to the pure pieces of the associated filtered φ‑modules. This perspective underlies the p‑adic local Langlands correspondence for GL₂(ℚp), where the Tannakian category of (φ, Γ)-modules over the Robba ring is shown to be equivalent to the category of smooth representations of GL₂(ℚp) via the Colmez functor, and the Tannaka group of the (φ, Γ)-module category is the Langlands dual group.

An important technical point is that the fiber functor Dcris is not exact on the whole category of p‑adic Galois representations, but it becomes exact when restricted to the crystalline subcategory. This exactness is essential for the Tannakian formalism to apply. The Tannakian group of the crystalline category is a pro‑reductive group (in fact a torus times a finite group) because crystalline representations are semisimple after a finite extension. The unipotent radical appears only when one passes to the larger category of de Rham or potentially semistable representations, where extensions between crystalline representations give rise to non‑trivial unipotent parts. The Tannakian formalism thus provides a clean conceptual separation between the semisimple part (captured by the reductive quotient) and the extension data (captured by the unipotent radical), mirroring the situation for mixed motives over number fields.

The Tannakian Category of Representations of the Absolute Galois Group of a Local Field and the Local Langlands Correspondence

Let F be a non‑archimedean local field of characteristic zero. The category of continuous finite‑dimensional ℓ‑adic representations of the Weil–Deligne group W′F (or, equivalently, of the absolute Galois group GF with the appropriate monodromy operator) is a neutral Tannakian category over ℚℓ with the forgetful fiber functor. The Tannaka group of this fiber functor is the pro‑algebraic completion of the image of W′F in GLn for varying n. The local Langlands correspondence for a reductive group G over F predicts a bijection between irreducible smooth representations of G(F) and certain Langlands parameters, which are homomorphisms W′F → LG (the L‑group of G). In the Tannakian language, a Langlands parameter is exactly a tensor functor from the category of representations of LG to the category of representations of W′F. The Tannakian formalism then allows one to transfer the correspondence to an equivalence of categories: the category of smooth representations of G(F) with a suitable notion of tensor product (the Bernstein–Zelevinsky product) should be equivalent, as a Tannakian category, to the category of representations of W′F equipped with the additional structure of a homomorphism to LG. This viewpoint has been made precise for GLn by the work of Harris–Taylor and Henniart, where the category of smooth representations of GLn(F) is shown to be Tannakian with a fiber functor given by the Jacquet module, and the Tannaka group is the Langlands dual group GLn(ℂ). The local Langlands correspondence then becomes an equivalence of Tannakian categories, and the preservation of L‑functions and ε‑factors corresponds to the compatibility of the fiber functors. For general reductive groups, the Tannakian approach suggests that the category of representations of the L‑group should be the universal Tannakian category receiving a tensor functor from the category of Galois representations, and the local Langlands correspondence is the statement that this universal property is satisfied by the category of smooth representations of G(F) with the appropriate tensor structure.

A concrete illustration is the case of GL₂(F). The category of smooth admissible representations of GL₂(F) is not Tannakian with the usual tensor product, but after passing to the derived category and using the Bernstein–Zelevinsky product, one obtains a Tannakian category whose Tannaka group is GL₂(ℂ). The local Langlands correspondence for GL₂ then identifies the Tannaka group of the Galois side (the image of W′F in GL₂(ℚℓ)) with the Tannaka group of the automorphic side. The Tannakian formalism thus provides a structural explanation for the preservation of local factors: the L‑function of a representation is the determinant of the action of Frobenius on the fiber functor, and the ε‑factor is the comparison isomorphism between the two natural fiber functors (the standard one and the one twisted by the Weil–Deligne monodromy). This Tannakian interpretation is the foundation for the geometric Langlands program, where the role of the Galois group is played by the fundamental group of a curve and the role of the L‑group is played by the Langlands dual group of a reductive group over the function field.

The Tannakian Approach to the Theory of Periods: The Motivic Galois Group and the Conjecture of Kontsevich–Zagier

The Kontsevich–Zagier conjecture asserts that every period (in the sense of an integral of an algebraic differential form over a domain defined by algebraic inequalities) can be expressed as a ℚ-linear combination of periods of mixed motives, and that all algebraic relations among periods come from the Tannakian structure of the category of mixed motives. In the Tannakian formalism, the period torsor Pmot = Iso⊗(ωB, ωdR) is a torsor under the motivic Galois group Gmot. The coordinates of a point of Pmot corresponding to a motive M are precisely the entries of the period matrix of M. The conjecture of Kontsevich–Zagier is then equivalent to the statement that the natural map from the ring of periods (the subring of ℂ generated by all periods) to the coordinate ring of Pmot is an isomorphism. In other words, the Tannakian category of mixed motives captures exactly the algebraic relations among periods. The motivic Galois group Gmot acts on the period torsor, and the dimension of the orbit of a period point equals the transcendence degree of the field generated by the periods of the corresponding motive. The Grothendieck period conjecture, which is a special case of the Kontsevich–Zagier conjecture for motives, predicts that this orbit dimension is maximal, i.e. that the periods are algebraically independent except for the relations forced by the Tannakian structure (such as the relations coming from the tensor product and duality). For mixed Tate motives over ℤ, the period torsor is an affine space whose coordinates are the multiple zeta values ζ(n₁,… ,nr). The Tannakian group is a semi‑direct product of the multiplicative group 𝔾m (the grading) with a pro‑unipotent group whose Lie algebra is free on generators σ2k+1 in degree −(2k+1). The Kontsevich–Zagier conjecture in this setting reduces to the statement that the odd zeta values ζ(3), ζ(5), … are algebraically independent over ℚ and that all algebraic relations among multiple zeta values are generated by the double shuffle relations. This has been verified in low weights and is supported by extensive numerical evidence. The Tannakian formalism thus provides a precise algebraic framework for the study of periods, turning transcendence questions into questions about the dimension of orbits under the motivic Galois group.

An important consequence is that any algebraic relation among periods of a given motive M must be invariant under the action of the motivic Galois group of the Tannakian subcategory generated by M. This invariance forces the relation to be a consequence of the tensor identities (associativity, commutativity, duality) and the weight filtration. In particular, if a period appears as a coordinate of a point in the period torsor of a mixed Tate motive, its algebraic relations are exactly those coming from the Hopf algebra structure of the coordinate ring of the unipotent radical. The Tannakian approach therefore reduces the problem of classifying period relations to the problem of understanding the Lie algebra of the motivic Galois group, which is a purely algebraic object. This perspective has led to proofs of special cases of the period conjecture using the theory of motivic multiple zeta values and the action of the motivic Galois group on them.

  • Confusing the Tannaka group of the category of crystalline representations with the full Galois group; the former is a pro‑reductive quotient, not the whole GK.
  • Assuming the category of all de Rham representations is neutral Tannakian with the forgetful fiber functor; exactness fails without restricting to a suitable subcategory.
  • Treating the motivic fundamental group of a variety as a profinite group; it is a pro‑algebraic group over ℚ, and its ℚ‑points are not the étale fundamental group.
  • Believing the unipotent radical of the motivic Galois group is always free; it is free only for mixed Tate motives over ℤ, while for general mixed motives it has relations coming from the geometry of the base field.
  • Interpreting the Kontsevich–Zagier conjecture as a statement about individual periods rather than about the dimension of the period torsor as a Gmot-torsor.
  • Neglecting that the comparison isomorphism between Betti and de Rham realizations is a point of the period torsor, and that the Grothendieck period conjecture is precisely the statement that this point is generic.
  • Assuming the local Langlands correspondence for GLn gives a Tannakian equivalence with the usual tensor product of smooth representations; the correct tensor product is the Bernstein–Zelevinsky product, and the equivalence holds only after passing to the appropriate derived category.

The Universal Property of the Motivic Galois Group and its Lie Algebra

The Motivic Galois Group, denoted Gmot, is not merely an abstract construct but a fundamental object defined by a universal property within the framework of Tannakian categories. Specifically, Gmot is the automorphism group of the fiber functor from the category of mixed motives (MMk) over a base field k to the category of vector spaces over a chosen realization field (e.g., ℚ for Betti, ℚp for p-adic, or ℂ for de Rham). This universal property means that Gmot is the unique pro-algebraic group over ℚ (or ℚp, depending on the chosen realization) such that the category of mixed motives is equivalent to the category of finite-dimensional representations of Gmot. This equivalence is the core of the Tannaka-Krein reconstruction theorem applied to motives.

The structure of Gmot is rich and reflects the intricate nature of motives. It is a pro-algebraic group, meaning it is an inverse limit of affine algebraic groups. Its Lie algebra, Lie(Gmot), plays an even more central role in understanding the extensions between motives. The category of mixed motives is an abelian category, and the extension groups Ext1(M, N) between two motives M and N are crucial for understanding their structure. These extension groups are precisely encoded in the Lie algebra of Gmot. More specifically, the Lie algebra Lie(Gmot) is equipped with a weight filtration, W•Lie(Gmot), which corresponds to the weight filtration on motives. The graded pieces of this Lie algebra, GrWLie(Gmot), are related to the Lie algebras of the Galois groups of pure motives of specific weights.

For example, the Lie algebra of the unipotent radical of Gmot, often denoted 𝔤unip, is a graded Lie algebra whose generators correspond to fundamental periods and whose relations correspond to the algebraic relations among these periods. For mixed Tate motives over ℤ, 𝔤unip is a free graded Lie algebra on generators σ2k+1 in degree -(2k+1), which directly corresponds to the algebraic independence of odd zeta values and the double shuffle relations among multiple zeta values. For general mixed motives, Lie(Gmot) is more complex, reflecting the geometric and arithmetic complexity of the underlying varieties. The universal property ensures that any cohomology theory that is a fiber functor on MMk will give rise to a representation of Gmot, making Gmot the "universal symmetry group" for all motivic cohomology theories.

The action of Gmot on the various realization spaces (Betti, de Rham, p-adic, crystalline) provides the framework for the period conjectures. The comparison isomorphisms between these realizations are not canonical but form a torsor under Gmot. The structure of Gmot, particularly its unipotent radical, captures the non-trivial extensions between pure motives, which are precisely the mixed motives. Understanding Lie(Gmot) is thus equivalent to understanding all possible extensions between pure motives, providing a deep algebraic handle on the entire category of mixed motives.

Reconstructing Tannakian Categories over Non-Algebraically Closed Fields

The classical Tannaka-Krein reconstruction theorem often focuses on neutral Tannakian categories over an algebraically closed field k, where the fiber functor maps to Vectk. However, many significant categories in arithmetic geometry, such as the category of Galois representations or motives over a number field, are defined over a non-algebraically closed field. To handle these cases, the theory of Tannakian categories must incorporate descent theory.

A Tannakian category 𝒞 over a field k is not necessarily neutral if there is no fiber functor 𝜔: 𝒞 → Vectk. Instead, it might only admit a fiber functor to a category of representations of the absolute Galois group Gk = Gal(k̄/k) on vector spaces over k̄, i.e., 𝜔: 𝒞 → Repk̄(Gk). In such a scenario, the category 𝒞 is called a non-neutral Tannakian category. The reconstruction theorem for non-neutral categories states that 𝒞 is equivalent to the category of representations of a k-group scheme G, which is an affine group scheme defined over k. This group G is then reconstructed as the group of automorphisms of the fiber functor 𝜔, but now considered as a group scheme over k.

The key insight from descent theory is that a k-group scheme G can be specified by its base extension Gk̄ = G ×k k̄, together with an action of Gk on Gk̄ that satisfies certain cocycle conditions. This means that a non-neutral Tannakian category 𝒞 over k can be "neutralized" by base extension to k̄. That is, 𝒞k̄ = 𝒞 ⊗k k̄ becomes a neutral Tannakian category over k̄, equivalent to Repk̄(Gk̄). The original category 𝒞 is then recovered as the category of Gk-equivariant objects in 𝒞k̄. The group Gk̄ is the "geometric" Tannaka group, while G is the "arithmetic" Tannaka group.

For example, consider the category of representations of the absolute Galois group Gk on finite-dimensional vector spaces over k. This is a Tannakian category over k. Its Tannaka group is precisely Gk itself, viewed as a pro-finite group scheme over k. Another example is the category of pure motives over a number field k. While it is not neutral over k (there is no canonical fiber functor to Vectk), it becomes neutral over k̄. The reconstructed group G for this category is the Motivic Galois Group Gmot,k, which is a pro-algebraic group over k. Its k̄-points are the automorphisms of the Betti fiber functor, and the action of Gk on these automorphisms defines the k-structure on Gmot,k. This interplay between base field extension and Galois action is fundamental for applying Tannakian duality in arithmetic settings, where the base field is rarely algebraically closed.

The process of descent allows us to relate the properties of the Tannakian category over k to the properties of the reconstructed group scheme G over k, even when a direct fiber functor to Vectk is unavailable. This is crucial for understanding the arithmetic properties of objects like motives and Galois representations, as it connects their categorical structure to the algebraic structure of group schemes defined over the base field.

Applying Descent to a Tannakian Category
Consider a Tannakian category 𝒞 over ℚ, whose objects are representations of the absolute Galois group Gℚ = Gal(ℚ̄/ℚ) on finite-dimensional ℚ-vector spaces. This category is not neutral over ℚ because there is no canonical ℚ-linear fiber functor to Vectℚ that is exact and faithful. However, it is neutral over ℚ̄.

(a) Describe the fiber functor 𝜔 that makes 𝒞 neutral over ℚ̄.
(b) Explain how the absolute Galois group Gℚ acts on the reconstructed group scheme over ℚ̄, and what this implies for the k-structure of the Tannaka group.
(a) The category 𝒞 consists of pairs (V, ρ), where V is a finite-dimensional ℚ-vector space and ρ: Gℚ → GL(V) is a continuous representation. To make it neutral over ℚ̄, we need a fiber functor 𝜔: 𝒞 → Vectℚ̄. This is achieved by base extension. For an object (V, ρ) in 𝒞, we define 𝜔(V, ρ) = Vℚ̄ = V ⊗ℚ ℚ̄. The representation ρ extends naturally to a representation on Vℚ̄. The fiber functor 𝜔 is exact and faithful.

(b) The Tannaka-Krein reconstruction theorem applied to 𝒞ℚ̄ (the category of representations of Gℚ on ℚ̄-vector spaces) yields an affine group scheme Gℚ̄ over ℚ̄. This Gℚ̄ is precisely the absolute Galois group Gℚ itself, viewed as a pro-finite group scheme over ℚ̄. The action of Gℚ on Gℚ̄ is given by conjugation: for σ ∈ Gℚ and g ∈ Gℚ̄, σ(g) = σgσ-1. This action defines the ℚ-structure on Gℚ̄, allowing us to descend Gℚ̄ to a group scheme G over ℚ. In this specific case, the Tannaka group G over ℚ is precisely Gℚ itself, viewed as a pro-finite group scheme over ℚ. This demonstrates how the absolute Galois group, which acts on the fiber functor, also defines the k-structure of the reconstructed Tannaka group, ensuring that the category over k is equivalent to representations of a k-group scheme.
Distinguishing Neutral and Non-Neutral Categories
When encountering a problem involving Tannakian categories, always first identify the base field k and the target category of the fiber functor. If the fiber functor maps to Vectk, it's a neutral category, and the Tannaka group is a k-group scheme. If it maps to representations of a Galois group over k̄ (e.g., Repk̄(Gk)), it's a non-neutral category, and you'll need descent theory. The reconstructed group will still be a k-group scheme, but its construction involves the Galois action on its k̄-points. This distinction is crucial for correctly identifying the nature of the Tannaka group and its properties.

The Motivic Period Ring: Structure and Algebraic Relations

The theory of periods, which are complex numbers arising from integrals of algebraic differential forms over algebraic cycles, finds its most profound algebraic framework in the Tannakian category of mixed motives. The "motivic period ring" is a conceptual object that encapsulates all algebraic relations among periods. It is not a single ring in the classical sense but rather the coordinate ring of the period torsor, Pmot, which is a principal homogeneous space under the motivic Galois group Gmot.

Recall that Pmot = Iso⊗(ωB, ωdR) is the set of tensor-compatible isomorphisms between the Betti realization functor (ωB: MMk → Vectℚ) and the de Rham realization functor (ωdR: MMk → Vectk). A point in Pmot is a choice of comparison isomorphism, which for a given motive M, yields the period matrix of M. The ring of periods, denoted ℛ, is the subring of ℂ generated by all such period entries. The Kontsevich-Zagier conjecture, in its most general form, posits that the natural map from ℛ to the coordinate ring of Pmot is an isomorphism. This means that all algebraic relations among periods are consequences of the Tannakian structure of the category of mixed motives.

The coordinate ring of Pmot, often denoted ℚ[Pmot], is a highly structured object. It inherits filtrations from the category of mixed motives, notably the weight filtration and the Hodge filtration. The weight filtration on ℚ[Pmot] arises from the weight filtration on the Lie algebra of Gmot. The Lie algebra Lie(Gmot) is itself a graded object, and its structure directly dictates the algebraic relations among periods. For instance, the generators of Lie(Gmot) correspond to "primitive" periods, and the Lie brackets correspond to algebraic relations (e.g., product rules, double shuffle relations for multiple zeta values). The unipotent radical of Gmot, Gmotunip, is particularly important, as its coordinate ring captures the relations among periods of mixed Tate motives.

The Grothendieck period conjecture, a special case, states that the transcendence degree of the field generated by the periods of a motive M is equal to the dimension of the orbit of M's period point under the action of Gmot. This dimension is maximal, meaning periods are algebraically independent unless forced by the Tannakian structure. The Tannakian formalism provides the precise algebraic framework for these "forced" relations. Any algebraic relation among periods of a given motive M must be invariant under the action of the motivic Galois group of the Tannakian subcategory generated by M. This invariance forces the relation to be a consequence of the tensor identities (associativity, commutativity, duality) and the weight filtration.

Thus, the motivic period ring is not just a collection of numbers but a coordinate ring whose algebraic structure is entirely determined by the Lie algebra of the motivic Galois group. This transforms the problem of understanding algebraic relations among periods from a transcendental number theory problem into a purely algebraic problem about the structure of Lie(Gmot), a pro-algebraic Lie algebra over ℚ.

Tannakian Duality as a Classification Principle for Pro-Algebraic Groups

The Tannaka-Krein reconstruction theorem is more than just a method to recover a group from its representations; it establishes a profound equivalence that serves as a classification principle for a wide class of algebraic groups. Specifically, it states that the functor from the category of affine group schemes over a field k to the category of neutral Tannakian categories over k (equipped with a fiber functor to Vectk) is an equivalence of categories. This means there is a one-to-one correspondence between affine group schemes and neutral Tannakian categories.

This equivalence provides a powerful "dictionary" between properties of an affine group scheme G and properties of its category of finite-dimensional representations, Rep(G). For example:

  • G is connected if and only if Rep(G) is generated by its simple objects.
  • G is reductive if and only if Rep(G) is semi-simple (every object is a direct sum of simple objects).
  • G is pro-unipotent if and only if Rep(G) is a unipotent category (every object has a unique simple quotient and a unique simple subobject, both trivial).
  • The dimension of G corresponds to the complexity of the tensor products in Rep(G).
  • The Lie algebra of G can be reconstructed from the infinitesimal automorphisms of the fiber functor.

This classification is particularly significant because it allows mathematicians to study complex algebraic groups by examining their representation categories, which can sometimes be more accessible or have a more natural geometric interpretation. Conversely, it provides a concrete realization for abstractly defined Tannakian categories as categories of representations of actual group schemes.

For instance, the category of mixed motives over ℚ, equipped with the Betti realization functor, is a neutral Tannakian category over ℚ. The reconstruction theorem then guarantees the existence of a unique pro-algebraic group Gmot over ℚ such that MMℚ is equivalent to Rep(Gmot). This provides an intrinsic definition of Gmot, independent of any specific realization. Similarly, in differential Galois theory, the Tannakian category of differential modules over a differential field (with a suitable fiber functor) is equivalent to the category of representations of a differential Galois group, which is an affine algebraic group. This establishes a direct link between the algebraic properties of differential equations and the structure of their symmetry groups.

The classification principle extends beyond affine group schemes to pro-algebraic groups, which are inverse limits of affine algebraic groups. This is crucial for applications in arithmetic geometry, where the relevant groups (like the motivic Galois group or the absolute Galois group) are often pro-algebraic. The Tannakian framework thus offers a universal language for describing symmetries, whether they arise from algebraic geometry, differential equations, or arithmetic, by equating them with the structure of their representation categories. This duality simplifies many problems by allowing translation between the language of groups and the language of categories, leveraging the strengths of both perspectives.

Common Traps

  • Confusing the universal property with a mere definition: The Motivic Galois Group Gmot is not just *defined* as the automorphism group of a fiber functor; it is *characterized* by a universal property that makes it the unique pro-algebraic group whose representations are equivalent to the category of motives. This distinction is crucial for understanding its role as a "universal symmetry group."
  • Assuming all Tannakian categories are neutral: Neglecting the distinction between neutral and non-neutral Tannakian categories, especially when the base field is not algebraically closed. A category may be Tannakian but not neutral over its base field k, requiring base extension to k̄ and descent theory to reconstruct a k-group scheme.
  • Overlooking the role of the absolute Galois group in descent: When dealing with non-neutral categories over a field k, forgetting that the absolute Galois group Gk acts on the reconstructed group scheme over k̄, and this action is essential for defining the k-structure of the Tannaka group.
  • Simplifying the structure of the Motivic Period Ring: Viewing the ring of periods merely as a set of numbers rather than a coordinate ring of a torsor under Gmot. Its intricate structure, filtrations, and the encoding of algebraic relations via Lie(Gmot) are central to the Kontsevich-Zagier conjecture.
  • Misinterpreting the "classification" aspect: Thinking Tannakian duality only provides a way to *find* a group for a given category, rather than establishing a full equivalence of categories that allows for a two-way translation of properties between groups and their representation categories.
  • Ignoring the pro-algebraic nature of Tannaka groups in arithmetic: Assuming the reconstructed group is always a finite-dimensional algebraic group. In many arithmetic contexts (e.g., motives, Galois representations), the Tannaka group is an infinite-dimensional pro-algebraic group, which is an inverse limit of algebraic groups.
  • Believing the Lie algebra of Gmot is always free: While the Lie algebra of the unipotent radical of Gmot is free for mixed Tate motives over ℤ, for general mixed motives, it has non-trivial relations arising from the geometry of the underlying varieties and the base field.

The Tannakian Category of Crystals and the de Rham–Witt Complex

A crystal on a scheme X over a perfect field k of characteristic p is a sheaf of modules on the crystalline site Xcris equipped with an integrable connection satisfying a nilpotence condition. The category Crys(X/W(k)) of crystals of finite presentation on X relative to the ring of Witt vectors W(k) forms a rigid abelian tensor category over W(k) when X is proper and smooth. The fiber functor is given by evaluation at the trivial PD-thickening, taking a crystal to its value on the structure sheaf. This functor is exact and faithful, making Crys(X/W(k)) a neutral Tannakian category. The associated Tannaka group is the crystalline fundamental group scheme πcris(X, x), a pro-algebraic group over W(k) that classifies crystals with a trivialization at a base point. The de Rham–Witt complex WΩ•X of Illusie and Raynaud provides a canonical lift of the de Rham complex to characteristic zero. Its hypercohomology computes crystalline cohomology, and the Hodge–Witt filtration induces a weight filtration on the Tannakian category. The Frobenius endomorphism F on crystals corresponds to the action of the absolute Frobenius on the de Rham–Witt complex. The category of F-crystals (crystals with a Frobenius structure) is a Tannakian category over W(k) whose Tannaka group is the F-crystalline fundamental group. The slope filtration of Katz and the Dieudonné–Manin classification of F-isocrystals over a perfect field are recovered as the weight decomposition in this Tannakian setting. The convergence of the Hodge–Witt spectral sequence for smooth proper varieties is equivalent to the semisimplicity of the associated graded pieces of the weight filtration, a statement that lies at the heart of the crystalline analogue of the Hodge conjecture.

The Motivic Galois Group and the Mumford–Tate Group: Comparison and the Hodge Conjecture

For a smooth projective variety X over ℚ, the Betti realization functor HB from the category of pure motives Mℚpure to the category of polarizable ℚ-Hodge structures is a tensor functor. The Tannaka group of Mℚpure with respect to HB is the motivic Galois group Gmot. The Mumford–Tate group MT(X) of X is the Tannaka group of the Tannakian subcategory generated by the Hodge structure H•(X, ℚ) inside the category of polarizable Hodge structures. There is a natural surjection Gmot → MT(X) induced by the inclusion of Tannakian categories. The Hodge conjecture for X is equivalent to the statement that this surjection is an isomorphism when restricted to the neutral component, or more precisely, that the kernel is a pro-unipotent group whose Lie algebra is generated by the Hodge cycles that are not algebraic. The Tate conjecture for X over a number field K admits a similar formulation: the ℓ-adic realization gives a surjection Gmot → Gℓ(X), where Gℓ(X) is the ℓ-adic algebraic monodromy group (the Zariski closure of the image of the Galois representation). The Mumford–Tate conjecture asserts that the ℓ-adic monodromy group is the base change of the Mumford–Tate group to ℚℓ. In the Tannakian language, this means the ℓ-adic realization functor and the Betti realization functor define the same Tannaka group up to base change. The absolute Hodge cycles of Deligne provide a bridge: they are the cycles that are Hodge cycles for every embedding into ℂ, and they generate the Lie algebra of the kernel of Gmot → MT. The motivic Galois group thus sits in an exact sequence 1 → U → Gmot → MT → 1, where U is pro-unipotent and its Lie algebra is the space of absolute Hodge cycles modulo algebraic cycles. The standard conjectures imply that U is trivial, so Gmot ≅ MT.

Worked Example: Reconstructing the Mumford–Tate Group from a Hodge Structure
Let V = ℚ2 with Hodge decomposition V ⊗ ℂ = V1,0 ⊕ V0,1 where V1,0 = ℂ·(1, i) and V0,1 = ℂ·(1, −i). This is the Hodge structure of an elliptic curve with complex multiplication by ℚ(i). Determine the Mumford–Tate group MT(V) as a subgroup of GL(V).
The Hodge structure is defined by the homomorphism h: 𝕊 → GL2,ℝ where 𝕊 = Resℂ/ℝ 𝔾m is the Deligne torus. On ℝ-points, h(z) acts as multiplication by z on V1,0 and by z̄ on V0,1. In the basis (1, i), (1, −i), the action is diagonal: h(z) = diag(z, z̄). The Mumford–Tate group is the smallest ℚ-algebraic subgroup of GL2 whose ℝ-points contain the image of h. The image of h is the torus { diag(z, z̄) | z ∈ ℂ× } ≅ ℂ×. Over ℚ, this torus is the restriction of scalars Resℚ(i)/ℚ 𝔾m, a two-dimensional torus. Its character group is ℤ[Gal(ℚ(i)/ℚ)] = ℤ ⊕ ℤ with the Galois action swapping the two summands. Thus MT(V) ≅ Resℚ(i)/ℚ 𝔾m. It is a proper subgroup of GL2 of dimension 2, reflecting the extra endomorphisms from complex multiplication. The motivic Galois group of the elliptic curve surjects onto this torus; the Hodge conjecture (known for CM elliptic curves) implies the surjection is an isomorphism on neutral components.
Exam Strategy: Tannakian Groups and Hodge Theory
When asked to compare motivic Galois groups and Mumford–Tate groups, always identify the relevant fiber functors (Betti vs. ℓ-adic) and the Tannakian subcategories they generate. The key exact sequence 1 → U → Gmot → MT → 1 is the standard template. Remember that U is pro-unipotent and its Lie algebra is the space of absolute Hodge cycles modulo algebraic cycles. The Hodge conjecture ⇔ U = 1. The Mumford–Tate conjecture ⇔ the ℓ-adic monodromy group equals the base change of MT. Do not confuse the Mumford–Tate group (a reductive group) with the full motivic Galois group (which may have a unipotent radical in the mixed case).

The Tannakian Formalism for the Category of Overconvergent F-Isocrystals and the Crew Conjecture

Let X be a smooth variety over a perfect field k of characteristic p. An overconvergent F-isocrystal on X is a crystal on the rigid analytic tube of X in a formal lifting, equipped with a Frobenius structure, satisfying an overconvergence condition along the boundary. The category Isoc†(X/K) of overconvergent F-isocrystals with coefficients in a finite extension K of the fraction field of W(k) is a rigid abelian tensor category over K. When X is proper, the category coincides with the category of convergent F-isocrystals, but for open varieties it is strictly larger and better behaved. The fiber functor is given by the rigid cohomology Hrig•(X/K), which is a Weil cohomology theory. The Tannaka group of this category is the overconvergent fundamental group π1†(X, x), a pro-algebraic group over K that classifies overconvergent F-isocrystals with a trivialization at a base point. The Crew conjecture (proved by Kedlaya for curves and by Tsuzuki in general) asserts that every overconvergent F-isocrystal on a smooth variety becomes trivial after a finite étale cover. In Tannakian terms, this means the overconvergent fundamental group is a pro-finite group scheme, i.e., its unipotent radical is trivial. The p-adic monodromy theorem of André, Kedlaya, and Mebkhout states that the local monodromy of an overconvergent F-isocrystal at a divisor with normal crossings is quasi-unipotent. This is the p-adic analogue of Grothendieck's quasi-unipotence theorem in ℓ-adic cohomology. The Tannakian category of overconvergent F-isocrystals is equivalent to the category of representations of the p-adic fundamental group of X in the sense of Fontaine, providing a p-adic analogue of the Riemann–Hilbert correspondence. The weight filtration on overconvergent F-isocrystals, constructed by Shiho using log-crystalline cohomology, makes the category a mixed Tannakian category, and the associated graded pieces are semisimple, reflecting the Weil conjectures for rigid cohomology.

The Tannakian Category of Representations of the Étale Fundamental Group and Grothendieck's Section Conjecture

Let X be a smooth geometrically connected variety over a field k of characteristic zero. The category Loc(X) of finite-dimensional ℚℓ-local systems on X (or ℚ-local systems for the topological fundamental group when k = ℂ) is a neutral Tannakian category over ℚℓ with fiber functor given by the stalk at a geometric point x̄. The Tannaka group is the ℓ-adic completion of the étale fundamental group π1ét(X, x̄)(ℓ).
