The Tannakian category of p-adic Hodge structures (filtered φ-modules, (φ, Γ)-modules, or Breuil–Kisin modules) provides the target for the p-adic realization functor. The category of filtered φ-modules over K is a neutral Tannakian category over ℚp with fiber functor given by the underlying ℚp-vector space. Its Tannakian fundamental group is the p-adic Hodge–Tate group, which is a subgroup of the p-adic motivic Galois group. The p-adic local Langlands correspondence for GL2(ℚp) (Colmez, 2010) can be interpreted as an equivalence between the Tannakian category of 2-dimensional p-adic representations of Gℚp that are de Rham with Hodge–Tate weights (0, 1) and the category of locally analytic representations of GL2(ℚp) of a certain type. In the Tannakian language, this is an equivalence of Tannakian categories that identifies the p-adic motivic Galois group of the relevant motives with a subgroup of the Langlands dual group. The p-adic period conjecture for the Tate motive ℚp(1) is equivalent to the statement that the p-adic logarithm map 𝒪K× ⊗ ℚp → K is injective, which is true. For an elliptic curve E over K with good ordinary reduction, the p-adic periods are the p-adic sigma function and the p-adic height pairing, and the p-adic period conjecture is related to the p-adic Birch–Swinnerton-Dyer conjecture of Mazur–Tate–Teitelbaum. The Tannakian formalism for motives over K also incorporates the theory of (φ, Γ)-modules: the category of (φ, Γ)-modules over the Robba ring is a neutral Tannakian category over ℚp whose Tannakian fundamental group is the p-adic local Langlands group. The Fontaine functor Dcris, Dst, DdR are tensor functors from the category of p-adic Galois representations to the corresponding categories of p-adic Hodge structures, and they induce homomorphisms of the respective Tannakian fundamental groups. The p-adic motivic Galois group Gmotp-adic(K) is thus a quotient of the absolute Galois group GK (via the p-adic realization) and maps to the p-adic Hodge–Tate group. The kernel of this map is the p-adic motivic fundamental group of the category of motives with trivial p-adic realization, which is conjecturally trivial (the p-adic conservativity conjecture). The p-adic period torsor Pmotp-adic is a Gmotp-adic(K)-torsor whose points correspond to splittings of the Hodge filtration on the de Rham realization compatible with the p-adic comparison isomorphism. The p-adic Grothendieck period conjecture asserts that the p-adic period map from the motivic period torsor to the p-adic period torsor is injective, i.e., the only algebraic relations among p-adic periods are the motivic ones. This is known for Tate motives and for CM motives via the work of Colmez on the p-adic Chowla–Selberg formula.

The Tannakian Category of Motives and the Theory of Motivic Heights: The Motivic Galois Group and Arakelov Theory

Let X be a smooth projective variety over a number field K. Arakelov theory provides a way to define intersection numbers on arithmetic varieties by adding "fibers at infinity" corresponding to the archimedean places of K. The arithmetic Chow groups CĤp(X) are extensions of the usual Chow groups by the group of Green currents. The motivic Galois group Gmot(X) of the motive h(X) acts on the motivic cohomology groups Hmoti(X, ℚ(j)), which are conjecturally related to the arithmetic Chow groups via the Beilinson regulator map Hmoti(X, ℚ(j)) → HDi(X(ℂ), ℝ(j)) (Deligne cohomology). The Tannakian category of mixed motives over K contains the category of pure motives as the heart of the weight filtration. The extension groups in the category of mixed motives, Ext1MM(ℚ(0), M) for a pure motive M, are related to the arithmetic Chow groups via the Abel–Jacobi map. For example, for an elliptic curve E over K, the group Ext1MM(ℚ(0), h1(E)(1)) is isomorphic to E(K) ⊗ ℚ, and the regulator map to Deligne cohomology is the Néron–Tate height pairing. The motivic height of a point P ∈ E(K) is the image of the corresponding extension class under the regulator map to the space of harmonic forms on E(ℂ). In the Tannakian framework, the motivic Galois group Gmot(E) acts on the space of motivic extensions, and the height pairing is the period pairing between the de Rham and Betti realizations of the universal extension. The arithmetic intersection theory of Gillet–Soulé defines a height pairing on the arithmetic Chow groups, and the motivic Galois group of the category of mixed motives over K acts on the arithmetic Chow groups via its action on the motivic cohomology. The arithmetic Kodaira–Spencer map relates the variation of the Hodge structure on the de Rham cohomology of a family of varieties to the motivic Galois group of the base. For a family of abelian varieties 𝒜 → S over a number field, the motivic Galois group of the generic fiber acts on the space of motivic normal functions (sections of the intermediate Jacobian fibration), and the height of a normal function is given by the Arakelov intersection number of the corresponding arithmetic cycle. The Tannakian category of mixed motives over K is conjecturally equivalent to the category of variations of mixed Hodge structure over K with good reduction at all finite places, and the motivic Galois group is the Tannakian fundamental group of this category. The Arakelov theory provides a metric on the determinant of cohomology (the Quillen metric), and the motivic Galois group acts on the space of metrics. The Faltings height of an abelian variety is the Arakelov degree of the determinant of the Hodge bundle, and it can be expressed as a period of the motive h1(A) in the Tannakian category. The motivic Galois group of the category of motives over K thus controls the algebraic relations among heights of cycles, special values of L-functions, and periods. The Beilinson–Bloch conjecture on the order of vanishing of L-functions at integer points is a statement about the dimension of the space of motivic extensions, which is the Lie algebra of the unipotent radical of the motivic Galois group. The Tannakian formalism for Arakelov theory was developed by Goncharov, Bloch, and others, who showed that the category of mixed motives over a number field is equivalent to the category of "motivic complexes" computing motivic cohomology, and the regulator map to Deligne cohomology is a tensor functor. The motivic Galois group acts on the motivic complexes, and the height pairing is the composition of the regulator with the cup product in Deligne cohomology. The arithmetic intersection product on CĤp(X) corresponds to the Yoneda product on extension groups in the category of mixed motives. The Tannakian category of mixed motives over K thus provides a categorical framework for Arakelov geometry, where the motivic Galois group is the group of symmetries of the arithmetic intersection theory.

  • Confusing the p-adic motivic Galois group (a pro-algebraic group over ℚp) with the p-adic Hodge–Tate group (a subgroup of GLn(ℚp)) or the p-adic local Langlands group; the former is the Tannakian fundamental group of the category of p-adic realizations of motives, the latter two are Tannakian fundamental groups of categories of p-adic Hodge structures or (φ, Γ)-modules.
  • Assuming the p-adic period conjecture for all motives follows from the Tannakian formalism; it is a deep transcendence statement equivalent to the injectivity of the p-adic period map on the motivic period torsor, known only for Tate motives and CM motives.
  • Thinking the elliptic motivic Galois group of a punctured elliptic curve is the same as the motivic Galois group of the elliptic curve itself; the former is a semi-direct product of the latter with a pro-unipotent radical whose Lie algebra is generated by Eisenstein series and Kronecker differentials.
  • Believing the CM motivic Galois group is always the full torus ResF/ℚ 𝔾m; it is the kernel of the norm map, and its dimension is g for a CM field of degree 2g.
  • Confusing the motivic height (a period of a motivic extension) with the Néron–Tate height (a real number); the former lives in the motivic period torsor, the latter is its image under the period map to ℝ.
  • Assuming the Tannakian category of mixed motives over a p-adic field is neutral without choosing a fiber functor; the p-adic étale realization gives a fiber functor to ℚp-vector spaces, but the de Rham realization gives a fiber functor to filtered K-vector spaces, and these are not equivalent as Tannakian categories without the comparison isomorphism.
  • Overlooking that the Grothendieck period conjecture for CM motives is a theorem in many cases (via algebraic independence of gamma values), while for non-CM motives it remains completely open; the Tannakian formalism does not distinguish these cases — it provides the framework in which both are formulated.

The Tannakian Category of Representations of the Motivic Fundamental Group of P1 {0,1,∞} and Multiple Zeta Values

The projective line minus three points, X = P1 {0,1,∞}, is the universal hyperbolic curve over ℚ. Its motivic fundamental groupoid π1mot(X; x, y) for tangential basepoints at 0, 1, ∞ forms a Tannakian category over ℚ whose fiber functor is given by the de Rham realization. The Tannakian fundamental group Gmot(X) is a pro-algebraic group whose Lie algebra is the free Lie algebra on two generators e0, e1 modulo the relation that the sum of the three monodromy operators vanishes. The category of finite-dimensional representations of Gmot(X) is equivalent to the category of unipotent vector bundles with integrable connection on X (the category of unipotent local systems).

The periods of this Tannakian category are precisely the multiple zeta values (MZVs) ζ(n1, …, nr) = ∑0<k1<⋯<kr k1−n1 ⋯ kr−nr. The de Rham fiber functor evaluates iterated integrals ∫01 ω1 ⋯ ωn where ωi ∈ {dt/t, dt/(1−t)}. The Betti fiber functor (with tangential basepoints) gives the same MZVs as real numbers. The comparison isomorphism between de Rham and Betti realizations is the period map, and its injectivity is the Grothendieck period conjecture for this category. The motivic Galois group Gmot(X) acts on the space of motivic MZVs, and the dimension of the weight-graded pieces of its Lie algebra is given by the Hoffman conjecture (proved by Brown): dim grW−n Lie(Gmot(X)) = dn where d0 = 1, d1 = 0, d2 = 1, and dn = dn−2 + dn−3 for n ≥ 3.

The Drinfeld associator ΦKZ(e0, e1) ∈ ℚ⟨⟨e0, e1⟩⟩ encodes the monodromy of the Knizhnik–Zamolodchikov connection and generates the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group GT(ℚ) as a subgroup of Aut(ℚ⟨⟨e0, e1⟩⟩). The Tannakian category Rep(Gmot(X)) is equivalent to the category of finite-dimensional representations of the pro-unipotent completion of GT(ℚ). The action of GT(ℚ) on the motivic fundamental groupoid gives the outer action of Gal(ℚ̄/ℚ) on π1mot(X), realizing the anabelian philosophy for this curve. The depth filtration on MZVs corresponds to the weight filtration on the motivic Lie algebra, and the double shuffle relations (stuffle and shuffle) are consequences of the Tannakian structure (coproduct and product on the motivic Hopf algebra of MZVs).

Tannakian Categories in Topological Quantum Field Theory: The Reshetikhin–Turaev Construction and Modular Tensor Categories

A modular tensor category (MTC) is a semisimple rigid braided tensor category over ℂ with finitely many simple objects, a non-degenerate S-matrix, and a twist isomorphism θV : V → V satisfying the ribbon axioms. Every MTC is a Tannakian category in the generalized sense: it is the category of representations of a quasi-triangular quasi-Hopf algebra (a quantum group at a root of unity). The Reshetikhin–Turaev (RT) construction assigns to an MTC C a (2+1)-dimensional topological quantum field theory ZC : Cob3 → Vectℂ, where Cob3 is the category of 3-dimensional cobordisms. The Tannakian formalism enters because the RT invariant of a closed 3-manifold M is computed as the categorical trace (quantum dimension) of the identity morphism on the object assigned to a Heegaard splitting surface.

The Drinfeld center Z(C) of a fusion category C is a modular tensor category, and the forgetful functor Z(C) → C is a tensor functor. When C = Rep(Uq(𝔤)) for q a root of unity, Z(C) is the category of representations of the quantum double D(Uq(𝔤)). The Tannakian reconstruction theorem for MTCs states that a braided tensor category with a fiber functor to Vectℂ is equivalent to Rep(H) for a quasi-triangular Hopf algebra H. The S-matrix Si,j = tr(cj,i ∘ ci,j) encodes the braiding, and its non-degeneracy is equivalent to the condition that the Müger center is trivial. The Verlinde formula Ni,jk = ∑l Si,l Sj,l Sk,l* / S0,l computes fusion coefficients from the S-matrix.

The Tannakian category of representations of the quantum group Uq(𝔰𝔩2) at q = eπi/r (the Lusztig quantum group) has simple objects V0, …, Vr−2 with quantum dimensions dimq(Vj) = sin((j+1)π/r)/sin(π/r). The RT invariant of the 3-sphere is 1, of the lens space L(p,q) is a Gauss sum, and of a knot complement gives the colored Jones polynomial. The Witten–Chern–Simons path integral with gauge group G at level k is conjecturally equivalent to the RT theory for the MTC Repk(G) (the category of integrable highest-weight modules of the affine Kac–Moody algebra ĝ at level k). The Tannakian formalism provides the algebraic backbone: the mapping class group representation on the space of conformal blocks factors through the action of the modular group SL2(ℤ) on the MTC via the S and T matrices.

Worked Example: Computing the S-matrix for the Ising MTC
The Ising modular tensor category has three simple objects: 1 (unit), σ (spin), ψ (fermion). Fusion rules: σ ⊗ σ = 1 ⊕ ψ, σ ⊗ ψ = σ, ψ ⊗ ψ = 1. Quantum dimensions: dim(1)=1, dim(σ)=√2, dim(ψ)=1. The twist eigenvalues are θ1=1, θσ=eπi/8, θψ=−1. Compute the S-matrix using the Verlinde formula and verify unitarity.
The total quantum dimension is D = √(12 + (√2)2 + 12) = 2. The S-matrix entries are Si,j = (1/D) ∑k Ni,jk dim(q)(k) θk / (θi θj). For i=j=1: S1,1 = 1/2. For i=1, j=σ: S1,σ = √2/2 = 1/√2. For i=1, j=ψ: S1,ψ = 1/2. For i=j=σ: Nσ,σ1=1, Nσ,σψ=1, so Sσ,σ = (1/2)(1·1·1 + 1·1·(−1))/(eπi/8)2 = 0. For i=σ, j=ψ: Sσ,ψ = (1/2)(1·√2·eπi/8)/(eπi/8·(−1)) = −1/√2. For i=j=ψ: Sψ,ψ = (1/2)(1·1·1)/(1) = 1/2. The S-matrix is (1/2)[[1, √2, 1], [√2, 0, −√2], [1, −√2, 1]]. Check S2 = C (charge conjugation): S2 = diag(1,1,1) since all objects are self-dual. Unitarity: S S† = I holds.
Exam Strategy: Recognizing Tannakian Structures in Physics Contexts
When a question mentions modular tensor categories, quantum groups at roots of unity, or 3-manifold invariants, identify the underlying Tannakian category: simple objects irreducible representations, fusion rules tensor product decomposition, S-matrix braiding eigenvalues, quantum dimensions categorical dimensions. The Verlinde formula is the Tannakian statement that the Grothendieck ring is diagonalized by the S-matrix. For Reshetikhin–Turaev, the invariant is the categorical trace of the identity on the state space assigned to a surface. Memorize the Ising MTC data (3 objects, dimensions 1, √2, 1) as the minimal non-trivial example.

The Tannakian Formalism for the Category of Mixed Motives over a General Base Scheme: The Relative Motivic Galois Group and the Weight Filtration

Let S be a regular Noetherian scheme of finite Krull dimension. The triangulated category of mixed motives DMgm(S) (Voevodsky) or the abelian category MM(S) (Nori, Ayoub) carries a weight structure (or weight filtration) whose heart is the category of pure motives PM(S). For a morphism f : X → S, the relative motivic Galois group Gmot(X/S) is the Tannakian fundamental group of the category of motives over X relative to S, i.e., the category of motives M ∈ DM(X) such that the pushforward f*M is a direct sum of Tate motives over S. This group fits into an exact sequence 1 → U → Gmot(X/S) → Gmot(S) → 1 where U is pro-unipotent and its Lie algebra is graded by the weight filtration: Lie(U) = ⊕n≥1 grW−n Lie(U).

The weight filtration on a mixed motive M over S induces a filtration on its realizations. For the ℓ-adic realization, the weight filtration coincides with the monodromy filtration on the inertia action (weight-monodromy conjecture). For the de Rham realization over a field of characteristic zero, the weight filtration is the Hodge filtration shifted by the weight. The relative motivic Galois group acts on the weight-graded pieces grWn M, which are pure motives of weight n. The extension groups Ext1MM(S)(ℚ(0), M) classify mixed motives with weight filtration 0 ⊂ W−1 ⊂ W0 = M where grW0 = ℚ(0) and grW−1 = M. These extensions are controlled by the Lie algebra of the unipotent radical of Gmot(S).

When S = Spec(k) for a number field k, the relative motivic Galois group of a variety X/k is the Tannakian fundamental group of the category of motives generated by the cohomology of X. The weight filtration on Hn(X) has graded pieces grWm Hn which are pure motives of weight m. The motivic Galois group Gmot(X) is a subgroup of the product of the motivic Galois groups of the graded pieces, and the obstruction to splitting the weight filtration lies in the cohomology of the unipotent radical. The conservativity conjecture (proved by Huber–Wildeshaus for motives over a field) states that the realization functors are conservative on the heart of the weight structure, implying that the weight filtration is the unique filtration with pure graded pieces stable under the motivic Galois group.

The Tannakian Category of Representations of the Étale Fundamental Group and the Grothendieck–Teichmüller Group

Let X be a smooth geometrically connected variety over a field k. The étale fundamental group π1ét(X, x̄) is a profinite group. The category of finite-dimensional continuous ℚℓ-representations of π1ét(X) is a neutral Tannakian category over ℚℓ with fiber functor the forgetful functor. Its Tannakian fundamental group is the Zariski closure of the image of π1ét(X) in GLn(ℚℓ) for varying n, i.e., the pro-algebraic completion π1ét(X)alg. When X = P1ℚ̄ {0,1,∞}, the outer action of Gal(ℚ̄/ℚ) on π1ét(X) gives a homomorphism Gal(ℚ̄/ℚ) → Out(π1ét(X)). The image lies in the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group GT(ℚ̂), which is the group of automorphisms of the profinite completion of the free group on two generators preserving the cyclotomic character and the Ihara relations.

The Tannakian category of ℚℓ-local systems on X is equivalent to Repℚℓ(π1ét(X)alg). The motivic fundamental group π1mot(X) maps to π1ét(X)alg via the ℓ-adic realization functor. The Grothendieck–Teichmüller group GT(ℚ) acts on the motivic fundamental groupoid of P1 {0,1,∞}, and its pro-unipotent completion is the motivic Galois group of the category of mixed Tate motives over ℤ. The section conjecture of Grothendieck asserts that for a hyperbolic curve X over a number field, the set of rational points X(k) injects into the set of conjugacy classes of sections of the exact sequence 1 → π1ét(X̄) → π1ét(X) → Gal(k̄/k) → 1. The Tannakian interpretation: a section corresponds to a fiber functor on the category of ℚℓ-local systems that is compatible with the Galois action.

The pro-algebraic completion of the topological fundamental group of a complex variety X(ℂ) is the Tannakian fundamental group of the category of ℂ-local systems (or equivalently, the category of unipotent vector bundles with integrable connection). The comparison isomorphism between the étale and de Rham fundamental groups is a Tannakian isomorphism of fiber functors. The motivic fundamental group π1mot(X) is the universal object mapping to both. The Lie algebra of the unipotent radical of π1mot(X) is the completion of the free Lie algebra on H1(X) modulo the ideal generated by the image of the cup product H1 ⊗ H1 → H2. For X = P1 {0,1,∞}, this is the free Lie algebra on two generators, and the motivic Galois group is the semi-direct product of Gm (weight grading) with the pro-unipotent group whose Lie algebra is the free Lie algebra on e0, e1 modulo the relation [e0, e1] + cyclic = 0.

Tannakian Categories and the Theory of Motives: The Motivic Galois Group and the Theory of Algebraic Cycles Modulo Homological and Numerical Equivalence

The category of pure motives PM(k) over a field k with respect to an adequate equivalence relation ∼ (rational, homological, numerical) is a rigid abelian tensor category. When ∼ = homological equivalence for a Weil cohomology theory H*, the realization functor H* : PM(k) → VectK is a fiber functor, making PM(k) a neutral Tannakian category. Its Tannakian fundamental group is the motivic Galois group Gmot(k, H*). The standard conjectures of Grothendieck (Lefschetz type, Künneth type, Hodge type) imply that homological equivalence equals numerical equivalence, and that PM(k) is a semisimple abelian category. In this case, Gmot(k) is a pro-reductive group, and its representation category is the category of pure motives.

The Hodge conjecture for a smooth projective variety X over ℂ is equivalent to the statement that the Hodge realization functor from the category of motives generated by h(X) to the category of Hodge structures is fully faithful. The Mumford–Tate group MT(X) is the Tannakian fundamental group of the category of Hodge structures generated by H*(X). The motivic Galois group Gmot(X) maps to MT(X), and the Hodge conjecture implies this map is surjective. The Tate conjecture for X over a finitely generated field k is equivalent to the surjectivity of the map Gmot(X) → Gℓ(X) where Gℓ(X) is the ℓ-adic Galois group (the Zariski closure of the Galois image on H*ét(X̄, ℚℓ)).

The category of motives modulo numerical equivalence PMnum(k) is conjecturally semisimple (Jannsen's theorem: it is semisimple if and only if numerical equivalence equals homological equivalence for all Weil cohomologies). The motivic Galois group for numerical equivalence is the maximal pro-reductive quotient of the motivic Galois group for homological equivalence. The Lie algebra of the unipotent radical of Gmot(k) is graded by the weight filtration, and its graded pieces are extension groups between pure motives. The Bloch–Beilinson filtration on Chow groups CHp(X) ⊗ ℚ is conjecturally the filtration induced by the weight filtration on the motivic cohomology, and the graded pieces are Ext groups in the category of mixed motives. The motivic Galois group acts on the Chow groups via its action on motivic cohomology, and the invariants under the unipotent radical give the Griffiths group (cycles homologous to zero modulo algebraic equivalence).

  • Confusing the motivic fundamental group of P1 {0,1,∞} (a pro-algebraic group over ℚ) with the étale fundamental group (a profinite group) or the topological fundamental group (a discrete group); the former is the Tannakian fundamental group of the category of mixed Tate motives over ℤ, the latter two are its ℓ-adic and de Rham realizations.
  • Assuming every modular tensor category comes from a quantum group at a root of unity; there are exotic MTCs (e.g., Haagerup, extended Haagerup) not arising from quantum groups, though all are Tannakian in the sense of being representation categories of weak Hopf algebras.
  • Thinking the relative motivic Galois group Gmot(X/S) is always a semi-direct product of Gmot(S) with a unipotent group; the extension may be non-split, and the obstruction lies in H2(Gmot(S), Lie(U)).
  • Believing the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group GT(ℚ) is the full motivic Galois group of mixed Tate motives over ℤ; it is the pro-unipotent completion of GT(ℚ), and the full group includes the weight grading Gm.
  • Confusing the motivic Galois group for homological equivalence (which depends on the choice of Weil cohomology) with the motivic Galois group for numerical equivalence (which is intrinsic and pro-reductive); the former maps onto the latter with unipotent kernel.
  • Assuming the weight filtration on a mixed motive splits canonically; it splits only after choosing a fiber functor, and the set of splittings is a torsor under the unipotent radical of the motivic Galois group.
  • Thinking the Reshetikhin–Turaev invariant of a 3-manifold is always a topological invariant independent of the framing; it depends on a choice of framing (or p1-structure), and the anomaly is corrected by the central charge of the MTC.

Tannakian Categories and the Theory of Motivic Cohomology Operations

The category of mixed motives over a field k, when equipped with a suitable realization functor, becomes a neutral Tannakian category whose fundamental group is the motivic Galois group Gmot(k). Motivic cohomology operations arise as natural transformations between the functors HiM(−, ℚ(j)) on the category of smooth varieties. In the Tannakian framework these operations correspond to elements of the Hopf algebra of functions on Gmot(k), and the composition of operations reflects the convolution product in that Hopf algebra. Voevodsky’s construction of the motivic Steenrod algebra shows that the reduced power operations act on motivic cohomology with finite coefficients, and the Tannakian formalism predicts that the algebra of all such operations is a quotient of the coordinate ring of a pro‑algebraic subgroup of Gmot(k). The weight filtration on mixed motives induces a grading on the motivic Steenrod algebra, and the associated graded pieces are governed by the Lie algebra of the unipotent radical of Gmot(k). This perspective explains why the motivic Steenrod algebra is much larger than its classical topological counterpart: the motivic Galois group contains a huge pro‑unipotent part coming from extensions of Tate motives.

Concretely, for a smooth scheme X over k the motivic cohomology groups Hp,qM(X, ℤ) are identified with ExtpMM(k)(ℚ(0), ℚ(q)[p]) in the triangulated category of mixed motives. The Yoneda product of extensions gives a ring structure compatible with the tensor product of motives, and the action of Gmot(k) on these Ext groups is exactly the action induced by the fiber functor. The motivic Steenrod operations are then recovered as the action of certain distinguished elements in the completed group algebra of Gmot(k) on the cohomology of the motivic Eilenberg–MacLane spectrum. This identification has been used by Hoyois, Kelly, and Østvær to compute the motivic Steenrod algebra over fields of characteristic zero and to relate it to the classical Steenrod algebra via the Betti realization functor.

The Tannakian Formalism for the Category of Motives with Coefficients in a Non‑Commutative Ring

When the coefficient ring R is a non‑commutative algebra (for instance a group algebra ℚ[Γ] or a matrix algebra), the usual definition of a rigid abelian tensor category must be replaced by a monoidal category enriched over R‑modules. The fiber functor then takes values in the category of finitely generated projective R‑modules, and the Tannakian reconstruction yields an affine groupoid scheme over Spec R rather than an affine group scheme. The resulting object is a Tannakian groupoid Gmot(k,R) whose representation category is equivalent to the category of motives with R‑coefficients. This formalism was developed by Deligne and Milne for the case of a commutative base and later extended by André and Kahn to non‑commutative coefficients using the language of 2‑categories. The key point is that the automorphism group of the fiber functor is now a sheaf of groupoids on the fpqc site of Spec R, and the reconstruction theorem asserts an equivalence between the original monoidal category and the category of representations of this groupoid.

An important application appears in the study of motives with coefficients in a number field E. Here R = E is commutative, but one can also consider the endomorphism algebra of a motive, which is often a division algebra D over E. The category of motives generated by such an object is naturally a D‑linear Tannakian category. Its Tannakian groupoid is a form of the motivic Galois group twisted by the Brauer class of D. This construction underlies the formulation of the non‑commutative Hodge conjecture and the non‑commutative Tate conjecture, where one asks whether the natural map from the motivic Galois groupoid to the ℓ‑adic Galois groupoid is an isomorphism after passing to the appropriate derived categories. The theory also interacts with the theory of motives over stacks, where the coefficient ring is the representation ring of a finite group acting on a variety.

Worked Example
Let k be a field of characteristic zero and let R = M2(ℚ) be the algebra of 2×2 matrices. Consider the category C of finite‑dimensional ℚ‑vector spaces equipped with a left R‑module structure, with tensor product over ℚ and the obvious R‑action on the tensor product. Show that C is a neutral Tannakian category over R and identify its Tannakian groupoid.
The forgetful functor ω : C → Proj‑R sending (V,ρ) to V regarded as a right R‑module via the transpose action is exact, faithful, and monoidal. Its automorphism groupoid Aut⊗(ω) is the sheaf of groupoids on Spec R whose objects over an R‑algebra A are the A‑linear tensor automorphisms of ωA. Because R is a central simple algebra, the Morita equivalence between ℚ‑vector spaces and R‑modules identifies C with the category of finite‑dimensional ℚ‑vector spaces. Under this equivalence ω corresponds to the usual fiber functor to ℚ‑vector spaces, whose Tannakian group is the trivial group. Hence Aut⊗(ω) is the trivial groupoid over Spec R, i.e. the groupoid with one object and only identity morphisms. The reconstruction theorem then gives an equivalence C ≅ RepR(1), which is exactly the original description of C.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for the Tannakian groupoid of a category with non‑commutative coefficients, first check whether the coefficient algebra is Morita‑equivalent to a commutative ring. If it is, reduce to the classical neutral case and then re‑interpret the result as a groupoid over the original base. This trick avoids heavy 2‑categorical machinery and is a standard shortcut in advanced problem sets.

Tannakian Categories in the Study of Periods of Calabi‑Yau Varieties

For a smooth projective Calabi‑Yau variety X over ℚ, the middle cohomology Hn(X) carries a pure Hodge structure of weight n with Hodge numbers hn,0 = h0,n = 1. The Tannakian subcategory ⟨Hn(X)⟩ generated by this Hodge structure inside the category of polarizable ℚ‑Hodge structures is neutral, with fiber functor given by the underlying ℚ‑vector space. Its Tannakian fundamental group is the Mumford–Tate group MT(X), a reductive subgroup of GL(Hn(X)). The periods of X are the entries of the period matrix comparing the Betti and de Rham realizations of Hn(X). The Grothendieck period conjecture for X predicts that the transcendence degree of the field generated by these periods equals the dimension of the MT(X)‑torsor of isomorphisms between the two fiber functors. In the Tannakian language this torsor is a principal homogeneous space for MT(X) over ℚ, and its ℚ‑points correspond to the possible comparison isomorphisms compatible with the tensor structure.

Recent work of André, Baldassarri, and others shows that the motivic Galois group Gmot(X) of the motive hn(X) surjects onto MT(X) with kernel a pro‑unipotent group U. The Lie algebra of U is graded by the weight filtration and its graded pieces are Ext groups between Tate twists of hn(X). The periods of X that are not accounted for by the Hodge structure lie in the coordinates of this unipotent part. In particular, the multiple zeta values appearing in the periods of certain Calabi‑Yau threefolds are interpreted as coordinates on the unipotent radical of Gmot(X). This gives a conceptual explanation for the appearance of multiple zeta values in mirror symmetry computations and connects the Tannakian formalism to the theory of motivic multiple zeta values developed by Brown.

The Tannakian Category of Representations of the Motivic Fundamental Group of a Higher Genus Curve

Let C be a smooth projective curve of genus g ≥ 2 over a field k of characteristic zero, and fix a base point x ∈ C(k). The motivic fundamental group π1mot(C,x) is the Tannakian fundamental group of the category of unipotent mixed motives on C with fiber at x. This category is a neutral Tannakian category over ℚ, and its objects are iterated extensions of Tate motives ℚ(n) by the motive h1(C). The group π1mot(C,x) is a pro‑algebraic group fitting into an exact sequence 1 → U → π1mot(C,x) → Gm → 1, where U is pro‑unipotent and the weight grading Gm acts by the natural scaling on the Lie algebra of U. The Lie algebra Lie(U) is the free Lie algebra on 2g generators modulo the single relation coming from the cup product pairing on H1(C). This description, due to Hain and Matsumoto, shows that the category of representations of π1mot(C,x) is equivalent to the category of finite‑dimensional representations of the completed group algebra of the topological fundamental group, but with the additional structure of a weight filtration and a mixed Hodge structure on each representation.

The de Rham realization of π1mot(C,x) yields the de Rham fundamental group, whose coordinate ring is the shuffle algebra on the space of holomorphic 1‑forms on C. The Betti realization gives the pro‑unipotent completion of the topological fundamental group. The comparison isomorphism between these two realizations is governed by the period matrix of C, and the Tannakian formalism predicts that the field generated by the entries of this period matrix has transcendence degree equal to the dimension of the motivic fundamental group. This is a higher‑genus analogue of the Grothendieck period conjecture for the Tate curve. The theory also provides a framework for the study of motivic iterated integrals on moduli spaces of curves, as developed by Brown and Levin, where the motivic fundamental groupoid of the moduli space acts on the motivic fundamental groups of the fibers.

Tannakian Duality for Tensor Categories with a Braiding

A braided tensor category is a tensor category equipped with a natural isomorphism cX,Y : X ⊗ Y → Y ⊗ X satisfying the hexagon axioms. When such a category is also rigid, abelian, and admits a fiber functor to vector spaces, the Tannakian reconstruction produces not just an affine group scheme but a quasi‑triangular Hopf algebra, i.e. a Hopf algebra equipped with a universal R‑matrix. The category of representations of this Hopf algebra is equivalent to the original braided category, and the braiding is recovered from the R‑matrix. This is the Tannakian duality for braided categories, first formulated by Majid and later refined by Deligne and Milne in the context of symmetric tensor categories. In the symmetric case the R‑matrix satisfies R21R = 1, and the resulting Hopf algebra is commutative, corresponding to an ordinary affine group scheme.

An important class of examples comes from the representation categories of quantum groups at generic parameters. These are braided but not symmetric, and their Tannakian duals are the quantum groups themselves, viewed as quasi‑triangular Hopf algebras. When the parameter is a root of unity, the category ceases to be semisimple but remains a finite tensor category; the reconstruction then yields a weak Hopf algebra or a Hopf algebroid. The Tannakian formalism for such categories underlies the Reshetikhin–Turaev construction of 3‑manifold invariants and the theory of modular tensor categories. In the motivic setting, the category of mixed Tate motives over ℤ is symmetric, but the category of mixed elliptic motives (motives built from an elliptic curve) is expected to be braided, with the braiding coming from the monodromy of the elliptic curve. The corresponding quasi‑triangular Hopf algebra would be an elliptic quantum group, and its representation theory would govern the elliptic multiple zeta values studied by Enriquez and others.

  • Confusing the motivic Steenrod algebra with the classical Steenrod algebra; the former is a quotient of the coordinate ring of a pro‑unipotent subgroup of the motivic Galois group, not a subalgebra of the classical one.
  • Assuming that a non‑commutative coefficient ring always forces a genuine groupoid; if the algebra is Morita‑equivalent to a commutative ring, the Tannakian groupoid is equivalent to an ordinary group scheme over the commutative base.
  • Thinking that the Mumford–Tate group of a Calabi‑Yau variety is always the full motivic Galois group; the motivic Galois group has a pro‑unipotent kernel whose periods account for multiple zeta values.
  • Believing that the motivic fundamental group of a higher genus curve is the pro‑unipotent completion of the topological fundamental group; it is an extension of Gm by that pro‑unipotent group, and the weight grading is essential.
  • Treating a braided Tannakian category as if its dual were an ordinary group scheme; the dual is a quasi‑triangular Hopf algebra, and the universal R‑matrix encodes the braiding.
  • Overlooking the role of the weight filtration in the Lie algebra of the unipotent radical; the graded pieces are Ext groups between pure motives, and this grading is what makes the period conjecture precise.
  • Assuming that the period torsor for a Calabi‑Yau variety is always a trivial torsor; it is trivial only if the Grothendieck period conjecture holds, which is unknown in general.

Super Tannakian Categories and the Reconstruction Theorem for Super Group Schemes

A super Tannakian category over a field k of characteristic zero is a rigid abelian symmetric tensor category C equipped with an exact faithful k-linear tensor functor ω : C → sVectk to the category of finite‑dimensional super vector spaces. The symmetry constraint in sVectk is given by the Koszul sign rule cV,W(v⊗w) = (−1)|v||w| w⊗v, where |·| denotes the parity. Deligne and Milne proved that the pair (C, ω) is equivalent to the category of finite‑dimensional representations of an affine super group scheme G over k. The reconstruction proceeds by forming the Hopf superalgebra O(G) = End⊗(ω) of tensor endomorphisms of the fiber functor; its even part is a commutative Hopf algebra and its odd part is a module over the even part. The parity shift functor Π on C corresponds to tensoring with the one‑dimensional odd representation of G. A crucial point is that the commutativity constraint in C is not the ordinary flip but the super flip; consequently the reconstructed super group scheme may have a non‑trivial odd Lie algebra. The theorem fails in positive characteristic because the super flip does not satisfy the hexagon axioms when the characteristic divides 2, and Deligne’s theorem on Tannakian categories in characteristic zero does not extend to the super setting without additional hypotheses such as the existence of a super fiber functor to sVectk.

In practice, super Tannakian categories arise naturally from the representation theory of Lie superalgebras (e.g. gl(m|n), osp(m|2n)) and from the category of mixed Hodge modules with a weight grading that is only defined modulo 2. The period matrix of a super motive acquires signs determined by the parity of the underlying cohomology classes, and the Grothendieck period conjecture for super motives predicts that the transcendence degree of the field generated by these signed periods equals the dimension of the motivic super group. When a problem asks for the reconstruction of a super group scheme from a given super Tannakian category, one must first verify that the fiber functor lands in sVectk and respects the super symmetry; then compute the Hopf superalgebra of tensor endomorphisms, taking care to keep track of the parity of each generator.

Tannakian Formalism for Hopf Algebroids and Finite Tensor Categories

Not every finite tensor category admits a fiber functor to vector spaces; the obstruction is measured by the failure of the category to be Tannakian in the classical sense. Etingof, Nikshych and Ostrik showed that a finite tensor category C over an algebraically closed field k is equivalent to the category of finite‑dimensional comodules over a finite‑dimensional weak Hopf algebra H (or, more generally, a Hopf algebroid) if and only if C is pivotal and its Drinfeld center is a modular tensor category. The reconstruction theorem in this context replaces the affine group scheme by a weak Hopf algebra: the algebra of endomorphisms of the forgetful functor F : C → Vectk carries a canonical weak Hopf algebra structure, and C ≅ Comodfd(H). The coproduct is not necessarily unital, and the antipode satisfies a weakened axiom involving the source and target counital maps. When C is braided but not symmetric, the reconstructed weak Hopf algebra becomes quasi‑triangular, and its universal R‑matrix encodes the braiding. A key example is the representation category of the small quantum group uq(𝔰𝔩2) at a root of unity; it is a finite tensor category that is not semisimple, and its reconstruction yields the Lusztig quantum group, a Hopf algebra that is not a group scheme. In the modular case, the weak Hopf algebra is actually a Hopf algebra, and the category is equivalent to the representation category of a quantum group at a root of unity. The Tannakian formalism for Hopf algebroids also applies to the category of representations of a finite groupoid scheme, where the base algebra is the coordinate ring of the object scheme. Examiners often test the distinction between a genuine fiber functor (which yields an affine group scheme) and a quasi‑fiber functor (which yields a weak Hopf algebra or Hopf algebroid). The presence of a non‑trivial source‑target map is the hallmark of the latter.

Examiner's trap
A question states: "Let C be a finite tensor category over ℂ with a braiding. Explain why the Tannakian reconstruction of C does not produce an affine group scheme but a quasi‑triangular Hopf algebra."
The braiding in C is not symmetric; the symmetry constraint cX,Y does not satisfy cY,X∘cX,Y = id. In the reconstruction, the algebra of tensor endomorphisms of the forgetful functor inherits a coproduct that is coassociative but the commutativity of the coordinate ring is replaced by the quasi‑triangular structure: there exists an invertible element R ∈ H⊗H such that Δop(h) = RΔ(h)R−1. This R‑matrix encodes the braiding. Because R ≠ 1⊗1, the Hopf algebra is not commutative, hence its spectrum is not an affine group scheme but a quantum group. The correct answer must mention the failure of symmetry, the appearance of the universal R‑matrix, and the resulting non‑commutative Hopf algebra.
Exam‑day strategy
When a problem mentions a braided tensor category with a fiber functor, immediately check whether the braiding is symmetric. If it is not, the reconstruction yields a quasi‑triangular Hopf algebra, not an ordinary group scheme. Write down the defining property of the R‑matrix (Δop = RΔR−1) to show you recognise the structure.

Tannakian Formalism for Gerbes and the Band of a Gerbe

A gerbe 𝒢 over a scheme S banded by a sheaf of groups G (in the fppf topology) gives rise to a Tannakian category Rep(𝒢) whose objects are G-torsors over S equipped with a compatible action of the gerbe. The fiber functor is the forgetful functor to the category of G-torsors, which is a neutral Tannakian category over the base topos of S. The reconstruction theorem of Breen and Messing identifies the gerbe 𝒢 with the 2‑group of tensor automorphisms of this fiber functor. Concretely, the band G appears as the sheaf of automorphisms of the unit object in Rep(𝒢), and the gerbe structure is encoded by the non‑abelian cohomology class in H2(S, G) that classifies 𝒢. When G is a diagonalizable group scheme, the category Rep(𝒢) is equivalent to the category of comodules over a Hopf algebroid whose base is the coordinate ring of the band. This formalism is essential for the study of the motivic fundamental gerbe of a variety, where the band is the motivic Galois group and the gerbe classifies motivic torsors. A typical exam question asks to reconstruct the band from the Tannakian category: one computes the automorphism group of the fiber functor on the unit object, which recovers G, and then the gerbe class is the obstruction to the existence of a global section of the fiber functor.

Stacky Fundamental Groups and the Tannakian Category of Representations of a Stacky Fundamental Group

The stacky fundamental group π1st(X, x) of a connected scheme X with a geometric point x is a pro‑algebraic 2‑group (a groupoid in the category of affine group schemes) introduced by Noohi. Its representation category Rep(π1st(X, x)) consists of finite‑dimensional representations of the fundamental groupoid of the étale topos of X. This category is a neutral Tannakian category over the base field with fiber functor given by evaluation at x. The reconstruction theorem states that the Tannakian fundamental group of this category is precisely the stacky fundamental group. The key difference from the classical fundamental group scheme is that the stacky version retains the automorphisms of the fiber functor coming from the inertia stacks of X. In particular, if X is a Deligne–Mumford stack, the inertia groups at geometric points appear as central subgroups of the reconstructed pro‑algebraic group. The Tannakian formalism for stacky fundamental groups is used to formulate a non‑abelian version of the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group action on the fundamental groupoid of ℙ1 {0,1,∞}. When asked to compute the stacky fundamental group from a given Tannakian category, one must identify the inertia subgroups as the stabilisers of the fiber functor on objects corresponding to the stacky points.

Pro‑Algebraic Groupoids and the Tannakian Category of Representations of a Pro‑Algebraic Groupoid

A pro‑algebraic groupoid 𝒢 over a field k is a groupoid object in the category of affine k-schemes whose object and arrow schemes are projective limits of algebraic groupoids. The category Rep(𝒢) of finite‑dimensional representations of 𝒢 is a Tannakian category over k equipped with a fiber functor to the category of vector spaces over the base scheme of objects. The reconstruction theorem of Deligne and Milne for groupoids asserts that the Tannakian fundamental groupoid of Rep(𝒢) is canonically isomorphic to 𝒢. The fiber functor is not a single functor to Vectk but a family of functors indexed by the objects of 𝒢, compatible with the groupoid structure. This formalism underlies the relative Tannakian duality used in the study of the motivic fundamental groupoid of a variety over a base scheme S. In that setting, the object scheme is the motivic Galois group over S, and the arrow scheme encodes the transport of motivic structures along paths in S. An examiner may ask to distinguish between a Tannakian category with a single fiber functor (giving an affine group scheme) and one with a fiber functor to a category of vector bundles over a base (giving a pro‑algebraic groupoid). The presence of a non‑trivial base scheme for the fiber functor is the decisive feature.

  • Confusing a super Tannakian category with an ordinary Tannakian category by ignoring the parity shift functor and the Koszul sign rule.
  • Assuming that every finite tensor category admits a fiber functor to vector spaces; the correct reconstruction yields a weak Hopf algebra or Hopf algebroid.
  • Treating the universal R‑matrix of a quasi‑triangular Hopf algebra as if it were the identity, thereby missing the braiding data.
  • Overlooking the band of a gerbe when reconstructing from its representation category; the band is the automorphism group of the unit object.
  • Identifying the stacky fundamental group with the ordinary fundamental group scheme and neglecting the inertia subgroups coming from the stack structure.
  • Forgetting that a pro‑algebraic groupoid requires a fiber functor to vector bundles over a base scheme, not a single fiber functor to vector spaces.
  • Misapplying Deligne's theorem in positive characteristic to super Tannakian categories without checking the characteristic‑zero hypothesis.

The Tannakian Category of Absolute Hodge Motives and Deligne's Theory of Absolute Hodge Cycles

Deligne's theory of absolute Hodge cycles provides a conditional Tannakian category of motives that avoids the standard conjectures. An absolute Hodge cycle on a smooth projective variety X over a field k ⊂ ℂ is a Hodge cycle that remains a Hodge cycle under every embedding σ: k ℂ. The category AHM(k) of absolute Hodge motives over k has objects (X, p, m) where X is smooth projective, p ∈ End(X) is an absolute Hodge projector, and m ∈ ℤ is a Tate twist. Morphisms are absolute Hodge cycles of the appropriate degree. Deligne proved that AHM(k) is a neutral Tannakian category over ℚ with fiber functor given by Betti cohomology HB(−, ℚ). The Tannakian fundamental group GAHk = Aut⊗(ωB) is the absolute Hodge Galois group. It fits into an exact sequence 1 → U → GAHk → Gmotk → 1 where Gmotk is the motivic Galois group (assuming the standard conjectures) and U is a pro-unipotent group encoding the failure of the Hodge conjecture. The key computational tool is the comparison isomorphism HdR(X/k) ⊗k ℂ ≃ HB(X, ℚ) ⊗ ℂ which identifies the de Rham fiber functor with the Betti fiber functor. The period matrix of an absolute Hodge motive has entries that are periods in the sense of Kontsevich–Zagier. Deligne's theorem states that every Hodge cycle on an abelian variety is absolute Hodge, so the category of motives generated by abelian varieties embeds fully faithfully into AHM(k). This gives a Tannakian interpretation of the Mumford–Tate group: for an abelian variety A, the Tannakian fundamental group of the subcategory generated by A is the Mumford–Tate group MT(A). The absolute Hodge formalism also applies to variations of Hodge structure: the category of polarizable variations of ℚ-Hodge structure over a smooth base S is a neutral Tannakian category over ℚ with fiber functor at a base point s ∈ S(ℂ), and its Tannakian fundamental group is the motivic fundamental group of S in the sense of absolute Hodge theory.

The Tannakian Formalism for the Category of Mixed Motives: The Weight Filtration, the Motivic t‑Structure, and the Theory of Extensions

The conjectural category MM(k) of mixed motives over a field k is expected to be a neutral Tannakian category over ℚ equipped with a weight filtration W• and a motivic t-structure whose heart is the category of pure motives PM(k). The weight filtration is a functorial increasing filtration W• on each object M such that grWnM is a pure motive of weight n. The motivic t-structure satisfies M ∈ MM(k)≤0 iff WnM = 0 for n > 0, and M ∈ MM(k)≥0 iff WnM = M for n < 0. The extension groups Ext1MM(k)(ℚ(0), ℚ(n)) for n ≥ 1 are conjecturally isomorphic to the motivic cohomology groups H1mot(Spec k, ℚ(n)) which, by the Beilinson–Soulé vanishing conjecture, vanish for n ≤ 0 and are finite-dimensional for n ≥ 1. For k a number field, the Bloch–Kato conjecture predicts Ext1MM(k)(ℚ(0), ℚ(n)) ⊗ ℚp ≃ H1f(k, ℚp(n)) where the right side is the Bloch–Kato Selmer group. The weight filtration induces a grading on the motivic Galois group Gmotk: the quotient by the unipotent radical U is the reductive group Gmot,redk acting on pure motives, and U is a pro-unipotent group whose Lie algebra is graded by weight: Lie(U) = ⊕n≥1 Lie(U)n with Lie(U)n dual to Ext1MM(k)(ℚ(0), ℚ(n)). The motivic t-structure is compatible with the six operations: for a morphism f: X → Y of smooth varieties, the functors f!, f*, f*, f! are t-exact up to shifts determined by the relative dimension. The conservativity conjecture asserts that the realization functors (Betti, de Rham, ℓ-adic) are conservative on the heart of the motivic t-structure. In the Nori category of mixed motives, the weight filtration exists unconditionally and the t-structure is defined by W•; the extension groups are computable via the Nori fundamental group scheme.

Worked Example: Computing an Extension Group in the Nori Category
Let k = ℚ and consider the Nori category MMNori(ℚ). Compute the extension group Ext1(ℚ(0), ℚ(1)) and identify its relation to the motivic fundamental group of ℙ1 {0, 1, ∞}.
In the Nori category, Ext1(ℚ(0), ℚ(1)) classifies mixed motives M with weight filtration 0 = W−1 ⊂ W0 = ℚ(0) ⊂ W1 = M and grW0 = ℚ(0), grW1 = ℚ(1). Such extensions correspond to elements of H1mot(ℚ, ℚ(1)) ≃ ℚ× ⊗ ℚ by the isomorphism H1mot(Spec ℚ, ℚ(1)) ≃ K1(ℚ) ⊗ ℚ ≃ ℚ× ⊗ ℚ. The motivic fundamental group πmot1(ℙ1 {0, 1, ∞}, x) is the Tannakian fundamental group of the subcategory generated by the Tate object ℚ(1) and the Kummer extensions. Its Lie algebra is the free Lie algebra on two generators e0, e1 corresponding to loops around 0 and 1, modulo the relation [e0, e1] = 0 in weight 2. The abelianization of πmot1 is ℚ(1) ⊕ ℚ(1), and Ext1(ℚ(0), ℚ(1)) appears as the weight-1 graded piece of the unipotent radical. Concretely, the extension corresponding to a ∈ ℚ× is the motive of the Kummer sheaf 𝒦a on 𝔾m with monodromy a at 0 and 1 at ∞. The period of this extension is log(a), and the motivic Galois group acts on the fiber functor by scaling the logarithm. The full extension group Ext1(ℚ(0), ℚ(1)) in MMNori(ℚ) is isomorphic to ℚ× ⊗ ℚ, and the regulator map to Deligne cohomology sends a to log|a|.
Exam Strategy: Weight Filtration and Extensions
When a question asks for the weight filtration on a mixed motive, first identify the pure graded pieces via the realization functors. The weight filtration is uniquely determined by the requirement that grWn is pure of weight n. For extension computations, use the isomorphism Ext1(ℚ(0), ℚ(n)) ≃ H1mot(k, ℚ(n)) and the known values: for n = 1 it is k× ⊗ ℚ, for n = 2 it relates to K2(k) ⊗ ℚ, and for n ≥ 3 it is conjecturally given by the Bloch–Kato Selmer groups. Remember that the motivic t-structure is the unique t-structure whose heart is the category of pure motives and whose truncation functors are given by the weight filtration.

Tannakian Categories and the Theory of Periods: The Period Matrix, the Grothendieck Period Conjecture, and the Theory of Regulators

The period matrix of a motive M over a number field k is the matrix of the comparison isomorphism compB,dR: HdR(M) ⊗k ℂ → HB(M) ⊗ ℂ with respect to bases of the de Rham and Betti realizations. Its entries are periods in the sense of Kontsevich–Zagier: integrals of algebraic differential forms over topological cycles. The Grothendieck period conjecture asserts that for a motive M in the Tannakian category M(k) of pure motives (assuming the standard conjectures), the transcendence degree of the field generated by the periods of M over ℚ equals the dimension of the motivic Galois group Gmot(M) of the Tannakian subcategory generated by M. Equivalently, the period torsor PM = Isom⊗(ωdR, ωB) is a Gmot(M)-torsor over ℚ, and the conjecture says the natural map PM(ℚ) → PM(ℂ) is a bijection on connected components. The theory of regulators provides a bridge between motivic cohomology and periods. For a smooth variety X over ℚ, the Beilinson regulator is a map rD: Himot(X, ℚ(j)) → HiD(X(ℂ), ℝ(j)) to Deligne cohomology. When i = 1, j = n ≥ 1, this maps K2n−1(X) ⊗ ℚ to extensions of mixed Hodge structures 0 → ℚ(n) → H → ℚ(0) → 0 whose period matrix involves special values of L-functions. The Bloch–Kato conjecture predicts that the regulator map on H1mot(Spec k, ℚ(n)) has image equal to the p-adic Selmer group under the p-adic comparison isomorphism. In the Tannakian language, the regulator is a morphism of fiber functors from the motivic fiber functor to the Deligne cohomology fiber functor, and its compatibility with the Tannakian structure implies that the motivic Galois group acts on the regulator values. The period conjecture for 1-motives (proved by Bertolin) states that the period matrix of a 1-motive M = [Y → G] (where Y is a lattice and G a semi-abelian variety) has transcendence degree equal to dim Gmot(M). For elliptic curves with complex multiplication, the periods are expressible in terms of gamma functions, and the motivic Galois group is a torus of dimension 2.

The Tannakian Category of Nori Motives and the Theory of the Motivic Fundamental Group Scheme

Nori's category MMNori(k) of mixed motives over a field k is constructed as the category of finite-dimensional representations of a pro-algebraic group scheme πN(X, x) called the Nori fundamental group scheme. For a smooth variety X over k with a rational point x, the category MMNori(X) is defined as the category of diagrams of finite-dimensional vector spaces with certain exactness properties, and the fiber functor ωx evaluates at the point x. The Tannakian fundamental group πN(X, x) = Aut⊗(ωx) is a pro-algebraic group scheme over k that classifies Nori motives on X. Its Lie algebra is the free Lie algebra on the set of points of X modulo the relations coming from the geometry of X. For X = ℙ1 {0, 1, ∞}, the Nori fundamental group scheme is the pro-unipotent completion of the topological fundamental group, and its coordinate ring is the shuffle algebra on two generators. The weight filtration on MMNori(X) is induced by the lower central series of πN(X, x): W−n consists of representations that factor through the n-th quotient of the lower central series. The motivic fundamental group scheme fits into an exact sequence 1 → UN → πN(X, x) → πét(X, x)alg → 1 where πét(X, x)alg is the pro-algebraic completion of the étale fundamental group and UN is the pro-unipotent radical. The category MMNori(k) for k a number field is equivalent to the category of representations of πN(Spec k, x), and the motivic Galois group Gmotk is a quotient of πN(Spec k, x). The key computational advantage of Nori's construction is that it works unconditionally over any field and does not require the standard conjectures. The extension groups ExtiMMNori(k)(ℚ(0), ℚ(n)) are computable via the cohomology of the Lie algebra of UN. For k = ℚ, the weight-graded pieces of Lie(UN) are related to multiple zeta values: the dimension of the weight-n piece equals the number of indecomposable multiple zeta values of weight n.

The Tannakian Formalism for the Category of Motives over a General Base: The Relative Motivic Galois Group and the Theory of Specializations

When the base is a scheme S rather than a field, the Tannakian formalism must be relativized. The category M(S) of motives over S (in the sense of Voevodsky, Ayoub, or Nori) is a Tannakian category over the base ring R = End(𝟙) with a fiber functor to the category of vector bundles over S. The Tannakian fundamental group is a pro-algebraic group scheme GmotS over S, called the relative motivic Galois group. For a morphism f: T → S, the pullback functor f*: M(S) → M(T) induces a morphism GmotT → f*GmotS of group schemes over T. The theory of specializations studies the behavior of motives and their Galois groups under base change to points s: Spec k → S. The specialization map sps: Gmotk → GmotS ×S Spec k is a morphism of pro-algebraic groups over k. When S is a smooth curve over a field k and η is the generic point, the specialization map at a closed point s relates the motivic Galois group of the generic fiber to that of the special fiber. The monodromy theorem in this context says that the action of the étale fundamental group π1(S, η) on the fiber functor ωη factors through the relative motivic Galois group GmotS. The weight filtration on M(S) is a filtration by Tannakian subcategories stable under specialization. The motivic t-structure on M(S) is compatible with the six operations and the specialization functors. For a family of abelian varieties A → S, the relative motivic Galois group is the Mumford–Tate group scheme over S, and the specialization map at s recovers the inclusion of the Mumford–Tate group of the special fiber into the generic Mumford–Tate group. The period torsor over S is a GmotS-torsor whose fiber at s is the period torsor of the specialized motive. The Grothendieck period conjecture relative to S asserts that the period torsor is trivial over the generic point if and only if it is trivial at all specializations.

  • Confusing the absolute Hodge Galois group with the motivic Galois group; the former is defined unconditionally via Deligne's theory, the latter assumes the standard conjectures.
  • Assuming the weight filtration on mixed motives splits canonically; it does not, and the extension data is encoded in the unipotent radical of the motivic Galois group.
  • Treating the period matrix as a single matrix rather than a torsor; the period torsor is a Gmot-torsor, and its triviality is the content of the period conjecture.
  • Overlooking that the Nori fundamental group scheme is pro-algebraic, not pro-finite; its coordinate ring is a Hopf algebra, not a profinite group algebra.
  • Neglecting the base scheme in relative Tannakian duality; the fiber functor lands in vector bundles over S, not vector spaces over a field.
  • Assuming the specialization map on motivic Galois groups is always injective; it can have a kernel coming from monodromy.
  • Confusing the Beilinson regulator (to Deligne cohomology) with the p-adic regulator (to p-adic étale cohomology); they live in different realization categories.

The Tannakian Category of Logarithmic Motives and the Logarithmic Motivic Galois Group

The extension of the Tannakian formalism to logarithmic geometry provides a framework for motives with controlled singularities along a normal crossings divisor. Let X be a smooth scheme over a field k of characteristic zero and D ⊂ X a strict normal crossings divisor. The category of logarithmic motives Mlog(X, D) is constructed as the heart of a motivic t-structure on the stable homotopy category of logarithmic motives SHlog(X, D) developed by Ayoub, Cisinski–Déglise, and others. It is a neutral Tannakian category over ℚ with a fiber functor given by the logarithmic de Rham realization ωdR,log : Mlog(X, D) → Vectℚ taking a logarithmic motive to its logarithmic de Rham cohomology with its Hodge and weight filtrations. The logarithmic motivic Galois group Gmot,log(X, D) is the automorphism group of this fiber functor; it is a pro-algebraic group over ℚ that fits into an exact sequence 1 → Ulog → Gmot,log(X, D) → Gmot(X D) → 1 where Ulog is a pro-unipotent group encoding the extension data coming from the logarithmic structure. The Lie algebra Lie(Ulog) is generated by the residue maps along the components of D and their iterated brackets, reflecting the combinatorics of the normal crossings divisor. For X = ℙ1 and D = {0, 1, ∞}, the category Mlog(ℙ1, {0, 1, ∞}) is equivalent to the category of mixed Tate motives over ℤ[1/2] unramified outside {2, 3}, and its motivic Galois group is the semi-direct product of the usual mixed Tate motivic Galois group with a pro-unipotent group whose Lie algebra is the free Lie algebra on two generators modulo the relation that the sum of the residues is zero. The period torsor for logarithmic motives is a Gmot,log-torsor whose triviality is equivalent to the logarithmic Grothendieck period conjecture, which implies the algebraic independence of multiple polylogarithms at special values. The weight filtration on Mlog(X, D) is induced by the logarithmic weight filtration on the logarithmic de Rham complex, and the graded pieces are pure logarithmic motives of weight n, which are direct sums of Tate twists of the logarithmic motive of the strata of D. The Hodge realization of a logarithmic motive carries a mixed Hodge structure with a logarithmic Hodge filtration, and the comparison isomorphism between logarithmic de Rham and logarithmic Betti cohomology is an isomorphism of filtered vector spaces compatible with the weight filtrations. The Tannakian formalism for logarithmic motives allows one to define the logarithmic motivic fundamental group scheme π1,logmot(X, D, x) for a base point x ∈ X D, which classifies logarithmic motives with a fiber functor at x; its coordinate ring is the shuffle algebra on the set of logarithmic differential forms with poles along D, and its pro-unipotent completion is the Nori fundamental group scheme of the pair (X, D).

Tannakian Categories and the Theory of Motivic Nearby Cycles and Vanishing Cycles

The theory of motivic nearby cycles and vanishing cycles provides a Tannakian description of the monodromy action on the cohomology of a degenerating family of varieties. Let S = Spec R be the spectrum of a henselian discrete valuation ring with generic point η and closed point s, and let f : X → S be a proper morphism with X regular and the special fiber Xs a strict normal crossings divisor. The category of motives over S in the sense of Voevodsky or Ayoub admits a pair of adjoint functors Ψf : M(η) → M(s) and Φf : M(η) → M(s) called the motivic nearby cycles and motivic vanishing cycles functors. These functors are exact tensor functors between the Tannakian categories M(η) and M(s) when equipped with suitable fiber functors. The nearby cycles functor Ψf carries a natural action of the inertia group I ⊂ Gal(ηsep/η), and the vanishing cycles functor Φf fits into a distinguished triangle Φf(M) → Ψf(M) → i*(M) → Φf(M)[1] in the derived category of motives over s, where i : Xs → X is the inclusion. The monodromy operator N = log(T) where T is a topological generator of the tame inertia quotient acts on Ψf(M) as a nilpotent endomorphism, and the weight filtration on Ψf(M) is the monodromy weight filtration centered at the weight of M. The Tannakian formalism implies that the category of motives over η equipped with the nearby cycles functor is equivalent to the category of representations of a pro-algebraic group Gmot,Ψ which is an extension of the motivic Galois group Gmot(η) by a pro-unipotent group encoding the monodromy action. The motivic monodromy theorem asserts that the action of the wild inertia subgroup on Ψf(M) is unipotent, and the tame inertia acts through a finite quotient. The weight spectral sequence for the special fiber Xs = ∪i Di degenerates at E2 and computes the weight filtration on Ψf(M) in terms of the motives of the strata DI = ∩i∈I Di. The motivic Clemens–Schmid exact sequence relates the vanishing cycles, nearby cycles, and the specialization map on motivic cohomology. For a semistable family of abelian varieties, the motivic nearby cycles functor recovers the Mumford–Tate group of the generic fiber and its limit mixed Hodge structure, and the monodromy operator N corresponds to the logarithm of the monodromy transformation on the Tate module. The Tannakian category of motives with a nilpotent endomorphism (the category of N-motives) is equivalent to the category of representations of the semi-direct product Gmot ⋉ Ga where Ga acts via the weight filtration. The motivic vanishing cycles functor Φf is a tensor functor to the category of motives over s with a nilpotent endomorphism, and its essential image consists of motives whose monodromy weight filtration is centered at weight −1. The theory of motivic nearby cycles provides a Tannakian interpretation of the local monodromy theorem of Grothendieck and the weight monodromy conjecture of Rapoport–Zink.

Worked Example: Computing the Logarithmic Motivic Galois Group of ℙ¹{0,1,∞}
Let X = ℙ¹ℚ and D = {0, 1, ∞}. Describe the logarithmic motivic Galois group Gmot,log(X, D) and its action on the fiber functor ωdR,log. Compute the dimension of the weight-graded piece Lie(Ulog)−n for n = 1, 2, 3.
The category Mlog(ℙ¹, {0, 1, ∞}) is the category of mixed Tate motives over ℤ[1/2] unramified outside {2, 3}. Its de Rham fiber functor ωdR,log takes a motive to its logarithmic de Rham cohomology. The logarithmic motivic Galois group fits into 1 → Ulog → Gmot,log → GMT → 1 where GMT = Gm ⋉ UMT is the mixed Tate motivic Galois group over ℤ[1/2]. The pro-unipotent radical Ulog is the kernel of the map to GMT; its Lie algebra Lie(Ulog) is the free Lie algebra on two generators e0, e1 (corresponding to the logarithmic differential forms dz/z and dz/(z−1)) modulo the relation e0 + e1 + e∞ = 0 where e∞ corresponds to the residue at ∞. Since e∞ = −e0 − e1, the Lie algebra is free on e0, e1. The weight grading is given by the length of the Lie word: weight −n corresponds to words of length n. For n = 1: basis {e0, e1}, dimension 2. For n = 2: basis { [e0, e1] }, dimension 1. For n = 3: basis { [e0, [e0, e1]], [e1, [e0, e1]] }, dimension 2. These dimensions match the number of indecomposable multiple zeta values of weight n (conjecturally). The action on ωdR,log is given by the iterated integrals of logarithmic forms, and the period matrix entries are multiple polylogarithms evaluated at 1, i.e., multiple zeta values.
Exam Strategy: Logarithmic vs. Classical Motivic Galois Groups
When a question asks for the motivic Galois group of a pair (X, D), always check whether the fiber functor is the classical de Rham functor on X D or the logarithmic de Rham functor on (X, D). The former gives Gmot(X D); the latter gives the extension by Ulog. The Lie algebra of Ulog is generated by residues along D — count the components of D minus the number of relations from the global residue theorem. For ℙ¹ with r points removed, dim Lie(Ulog)−1 = r − 1. This is a quick way to verify the weight-graded dimensions without computing the full free Lie algebra.

The Tannakian Formalism for Motives with Torsion Coefficients and the Étale Motivic Galois Group

The extension of the Tannakian formalism to motives with torsion coefficients requires working over a base ring that is not a field, typically ℤ/ℓn or ℤℓ. Let k be a field of characteristic different from a prime ℓ. The category of motives with ℤ/ℓn-coefficients, denoted M(k, ℤ/ℓn), is constructed as the heart of a motivic t-structure on the stable homotopy category of motives with coefficients in the E∞-ring spectrum representing ℤ/ℓn-cohomology. It is a Tannakian category over the base ring ℤ/ℓn in the sense of Deligne–Milne: it is a rigid abelian tensor category with a fiber functor to the category of finite locally free modules over ℤ/ℓn. The étale realization functor ωét : M(k, ℤ/ℓn) → Modℤ/ℓn(Gal(ksep/k)) taking a motive to its étale cohomology with ℤ/ℓn-coefficients is a tensor functor, but it is not a fiber functor in the strict sense because the target is not the category of modules over the base ring. However, after composing with the forgetful functor to Modℤ/ℓn, one obtains a fiber functor ωét,forget. The Tannakian fundamental group of M(k, ℤ/ℓn) with respect to this fiber functor is a pro-algebraic group scheme over ℤ/ℓn called the étale motivic Galois group Gmot,ét(k, ℤ/ℓn). It fits into an exact sequence 1 → Uét → Gmot,ét(k, ℤ/ℓn) → Gal(ksep/k)alg → 1 where Gal(ksep/k)alg is the pro-algebraic completion of the absolute Galois group over ℤ/ℓn and Uét is a pro-unipotent group scheme over ℤ/ℓn. The Lie algebra of Uét is related to the motivic Lie algebra of k with torsion coefficients. For k a number field, the category M(k, ℤ/ℓn) is expected to be equivalent to the category of continuous representations of the absolute Galois group Gal(ksep/k) on finite free ℤ/ℓn-modules that are de Rham at places above ℓ and unramified outside a finite set, but this equivalence is conditional on the Fontaine–Mazur conjecture and the Tate conjecture. The Tannakian formalism for torsion coefficients allows one to define the motivic Galois group of a motive with torsion coefficients as the image of Gmot,ét in the automorphism group of the étale realization. The weight filtration on M(k, ℤ/ℓn) is induced by the weight filtration on the étale realization via the weight-monodromy conjecture. The extension groups ExtiM(k, ℤ/ℓn)(ℤ/ℓn(0), ℤ/ℓn(j)) are related to the motivic cohomology groups Himot(k, ℤ/ℓn(j)) which are finite for i = 1, 2 and vanish for i > 2 by the Beilinson–Lichtenbaum conjecture (now a theorem of Voevodsky–Rost). The period torsor for motives with torsion coefficients is a Gmot,ét-torsor over ℤ/ℓn whose triviality is equivalent to the torsion part of the Grothendieck period conjecture. The Tannakian duality for categories over a ring (Deligne–Milne) implies that the category of representations of Gmot,ét on finite locally free ℤ/ℓn-modules is equivalent to M(k, ℤ/ℓn). The limit over n gives the category of motives with ℤℓ-coefficients, whose Tannakian fundamental group is the ℓ-adic motivic Galois group Gmot,ℓ(k), a pro-algebraic group scheme over ℤℓ.

Tannakian Categories in Factorization Homology and the Cobordism Hypothesis

The theory of factorization homology, developed by Beilinson–Drinfeld, Lurie, and Ayala–Francis, provides a topological framework in which Tannakian categories arise naturally as categories of boundary conditions for topological quantum field theories. Let C be a braided tensor category (e.g., the category of representations of a quantum group at a root of unity, or the category of perverse sheaves on a curve). The factorization homology ∫M C of a framed n-manifold M with coefficients in C is an En-algebra in the category of vector spaces. When C is a modular tensor category, the factorization homology of a closed 3-manifold M computes the Reshetikhin–Turaev invariant. The cobordism hypothesis of Baez–Dolan–Lurie classifies fully extended topological quantum field theories with values in a symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category D in terms of fully dualizable objects of D. For D = Cat(∞,n) the (∞,n)-category of (∞,n)-categories, a fully dualizable object is an En-fusion category. The Tannakian formalism enters when one considers the category of boundary conditions for a TQFT: if Z : Bordnfr → Vect is an n-dimensional framed TQFT, then the category of boundary conditions for Z is a Tannakian category whose Tannakian fundamental group is the automorphism group of the fiber functor given by evaluation on the interval. In the case of the 3-dimensional TQFT associated to a modular tensor category C (the Reshetikhin–Turaev TQFT), the category of boundary conditions is equivalent to the Drinfeld center Z(C), which is a modular tensor category itself. The Tannakian fundamental group of Z(C) with respect to the forgetful fiber functor to Vect is the quantum group Uq(g) at a root of unity, and the Tannakian reconstruction theorem recovers the modular tensor category from its fiber functor. For the 4-dimensional Crane–Yetter TQFT associated to a modular tensor category C, the category of boundary conditions is the category of module categories over C, which is a 2-category. The Tannakian formalism for 2-categories (developed by Douglas–Reutter) implies that the automorphism 2-group of the fiber 2-functor is a 2-group whose truncation is the Brauer–Picard group of C. The factorization homology of a surface Σ with coefficients in C is the category of C-modules on Σ, which is a Tannakian category over the category of local systems on Σ. The Tannakian fundamental group of this category is the mapping class group of Σ acting on the space of conformal blocks. The cobordism hypothesis implies that the space of TQFTs with a given value on the point is the space of fully dualizable objects in the target category, and the Tannakian formalism classifies the possible fiber functors on the category of boundary conditions. In the context of the geometric Langlands program, the category of D-modules on the moduli stack of G-bundles on a curve X is a Tannakian category over the category of coherent sheaves on the moduli stack of LG-local systems, and its Tannakian fundamental group is the automorphism group of the fiber functor given by the global sections functor. The factorization homology of X with coefficients in the category of representations of the affine Kac–Moody algebra at the critical level computes the category of D-modules on BunG(X), and the Tannakian formalism for this category is equivalent to the geometric Satake equivalence for the affine Grassmannian.

The Tannakian Category of Representations of the Motivic Galois Group of a Tannakian Category: Iterated Tannakian Duality

Given a neutral Tannakian category T over a field k with fiber functor ω : T → Vectk, its Tannakian fundamental group G = Aut⊗(ω) is an affine group scheme over k. The category Rep(G) of finite-dimensional representations of G is canonically equivalent to T by the Tannaka–Krein reconstruction theorem. One can then consider the Tannakian category Rep(G) with its forgetful fiber functor ωG : Rep(G) → Vectk. Its Tannakian fundamental group is Aut⊗(ωG), which is canonically isomorphic to G itself. This trivial iteration suggests that the Tannakian duality is an involution on the 2-category of Tannakian categories. However, a non-trivial iteration arises when one considers the category of representations of the motivic Galois group of a Tannakian category that is itself a category of motives. Let M be the category of mixed motives over a field k (assuming the standard conjectures), with motivic Galois group Gmot. The category Rep(Gmot) is equivalent to M. Now consider the category of motives over the classifying stack BGmot, or more precisely, the category of Gmot-equivariant motives over k. This category, denoted MGmot(k), is a Tannakian category over the representation ring Rep(Gmot). Its Tannakian fundamental group is the semi-direct product Gmot ⋉ Gmot where the action is the adjoint action. The iterated Tannakian duality thus produces a group that is an extension of Gmot by itself. More generally, for a Tannakian category T with fundamental group G, the category of G-equivariant objects in T (i.e., objects of T with a G-action compatible with the tensor structure) is a Tannakian category over Rep(G) whose fundamental group is G ⋉ G. The Lie algebra of this group is the semi-direct product Lie(G) ⋉ Lie(G) with the adjoint action. The weight filtration on this iterated category is induced by the weight filtration on T and the adjoint weight filtration on G. The period torsor for the iterated category is a (G ⋉ G)-torsor whose triviality is equivalent to the triviality of the period torsor for T together with the triviality of the action of G on the period torsor. This construction is related to the theory of motivic fundamental groupoids: the motivic fundamental groupoid of a variety X is a Tannakian category over the category of motives over X, and its Tannakian fundamental group is the motivic fundamental group scheme π1mot(X). The iterated Tannakian duality of the category of motives over X with respect to the motivic fundamental groupoid recovers the motivic fundamental groupoid of the motivic fundamental groupoid, which is related to the double loop space of X in the motivic homotopy category. The Tannakian formalism for iterated categories provides a framework for understanding the higher categorical structure of the motivic Galois group and its action on the category of motives. The category of representations of the iterated motivic Galois group Gmot ⋉ Gmot is equivalent to the category of mixed motives with a Gmot-action, which is the category of equivariant motives. The extension groups in this category compute the equivariant motivic cohomology groups, which are related to the motivic cohomology of the classifying space BGmot.

  • Confusing the logarithmic motivic Galois group with the classical motivic Galois group of the complement; the former is an extension of the latter by a pro-unipotent group encoding residues along the divisor.
  • Assuming the motivic nearby cycles functor is a tensor functor without verifying the monodromy weight filtration compatibility; the weight filtration on Ψf(M) is the monodromy weight filtration centered at the weight of M, not the naive weight filtration.
